#148. Shot in the face at 15: The lives shattered by Cameroon's decade of conflict

Median Bah was shot several times in the face by unknown gunmen

Median Bah was just 15 when her life changed forever.

She remembers the date vividly - 7 April 2018. She was ill and confined to her bed.

Armed men stormed her family's compound in Cameroon's Southwest region, one of the country's two English-speaking areas at the centre of a separatist conflict.

Median, now 23, says she was fast asleep when her brother woke her, panicked by the approach of a group of armed men who were shooting indiscriminately.

He escaped through a window. But for Median, fleeing was not so easy.

"Since I was sick, I had no strength to jump through the window with him, so I had to pass through the door," she tells the BBC.

That decision would have devastating consequences.

She says she came face to face with two of the attackers in the backyard, who opened fire at her.

They first shot her in the thigh before firing several more rounds, which severely damaged her face.

"I kept pleading with them not to kill me, that I wasn't feeling well. However, I found myself on the floor," she recalls.

Everything went dark. She lost consciousness and could neither see nor speak.

Median initially thought she was dreaming.

The attack left her with devastating facial injuries. Her jawbone was damaged, most of her teeth destroyed and much of her mouth severely injured.

The Anglophone crisis began in 2016, when English-speaking Cameroonians took up arms over alleged marginalisation by the French-speaking government (file photo)

Over the past eight years, she has undergone at least four reconstructive surgeries in Cameroon, Ghana and India, with more procedures expected next year.

Parts of her face have been reconstructed using tissue taken from other parts of her body.

She also lost the ability to breathe normally through her nose and mouth. She underwent a tracheostomy, a surgical procedure that creates an opening in the neck to allow air into the windpipe.

For eight years, she relied on a tube to breathe. It was only recently removed, allowing her to breathe normally again.

A teenager left with devastating facial injuries, a mother forced to flee her home and a student whose education was disrupted. Nearly a decade of fighting in Cameroon has left countless lives shattered.

The crisis began in October 2016, when lawyers and teachers in Cameroon's English-speaking regions went on strike over the alleged marginalisation of Anglophone communities by the Francophone-dominated government.

They were concerned about the appointment of French-speaking judges and teachers to courts and schools in the English-speaking Northwest and Southwest regions.

Initially, the protest was peaceful, with thousands demanding greater recognition of the English-speaking minority.

But human rights groups accused security forces of using excessive force to suppress the protests. The crackdown, critics say, helped fuel an armed separatist movement.

By 2017, a dozen armed groups had emerged, according to the International Crisis Group, seeking independence for the two English-speaking regions and the creation of a breakaway state known as Ambazonia.

Since then, separatist fighters have engaged in a prolonged and often brutal conflict with government forces.

Rights groups have documented serious abuses by both sides.

Separatist fighters have been accused of targeted killings, kidnappings for ransom, torture and attacks on schools and other public and private facilities.

Government forces, meanwhile, have faced allegations of arbitrary arrests and detention, torture and unlawful killings.

Both sides have denied the accusations, although in some instances they have acknowledged responsibility for abuses.

The violence has devastated communities and forced half a million people from their homes, according to data published by the UN this year.

At least 6,000 people have been killed since the conflict began, according to Human Rights Watch.

The UN refugee agency, UNHCR, says more than 70,000 Cameroonians have sought refuge in neighbouring Nigeria.

Gloria Akom is among 15,000 people who fled Cameroon for Nigeria's Cross River state

Glory Akom, 44, lives with a disability after surviving severe burns in an incident unrelated to the conflict.

The mother-of-two now lives in a camp in Ogoja, a town in Nigeria's south-eastern Cross River state, near the Cameroonian border.

Ogoja hosts about 15,000 refugees across three settlements, Richard Effiong, from the UNHCR, told the BBC.

The UNHCR has provided refugees with brick houses topped with tin roofs.

For many, however, uncertainty remains over when, or whether, they will ever return home.

Glory says her life in Cameroon was far better. She ran a business and was able to provide for her family.

But everything changed when fighting intensified in 2017.

She said soldiers deployed to her area in the Southwest region pursued residents into the bush, forcing them to abandon their homes and flee into Nigeria.

"We were sleeping in the bush, as we were always running," she said.

"If you slept in your house, they could kill you there."

Initially, she settled in a neighbouring village before moving to Ogoja, where she was eventually allocated a permanent home.

Like many other refugees, Glory depends on $67 (Â£51) monthly cash assistance from UNHCR to support her children, one of who suffers from sickle cell and requires regular medical attention.

The money is barely enough to cover food, medical bills and other necessities, she says.

Her sadness at having to leave home is shared by other Cameroonian refugees in Ogoja who spoke to the BBC.

Maurine Mangha, 21, said she and five other members of her family fled Akwaya in the Southwest region after separatist fighters opened fire during celebrations marking what they described as their independence on 1 October 2017.

"They shot a lot of people, and we had no choice but run into the bush," she tells the BBC.

Education in Cameroon's Southwest region has been severely disrupted by the conflict (file photo)

Maurine had stopped attending school months earlier because of a separatist-imposed education boycott.

The 2017 boycott campaign, which sought to pressure the government, involved threats and attacks against schools and those who defied orders to stay away.

The consequences for children have been severe.

More than 200,000 children are currently out of school in the conflict regions, while nearly 2,500 learning institutions remain non-operational in the two regions, according to the UN's children's agency, Unicef, citing Cameroonian education authorities.

Despite the disruption, Maurine managed to secure a scholarship through a higher education programme for refugees managed by the UNHCR.

She is now studying microbiology at a local polytechnic.

But Maurine says she cannot help thinking about friends who remained in Cameroon.

"I feel very bad for my friends as they did not have the opportunity [to further] their education because of the crisis," she says.

Maurine Mangha has managed to get a scholarship to continue her education

After nearly a decade of fighting, the Cameroonian government wants the separatists to abandon their armed struggle.

In September, the authorities launched a community dialogue initiative aimed at finding grassroots solutions to the conflict.

"It is you, all of us sitting here, who have the capacity today to organise to talk to those boys [separatists], to tell them, the time has come for them to leave," Prime Minister Joseph Dion Ngute said during the launch in Buea, capital of the Southwest region.

He repeated a similar message in Bamenda, the main city in the Northwest region and one of the epicentres of the conflict.

At both events, nearly 60 people described as former separatist fighters were presented as having abandoned the armed struggle and surrendered to the state.

As of November 2026, almost 1,500 former separatist fighters were undergoing rehabilitation at Disarmament, Demobilisation and Reintegration (DDR) centres in the two English-speaking regions, a Cameroonian official from the agency overseeing the programme told the BBC.

Although a similar initiative in 2019 failed to end the fighting, the government hopes to persuade more fighters to abandon their campaign for independence.

"A lot of Amba boys [separatist fighters] have been killed, others have surrendered, and many of them have abandoned the ideology of independence," says Clovis Naseri, a former separatist commander.

President Paul Biya, who has been in power since 1982, has faced criticism over his government's military crackdown on Anglophone separatists

Clovis spent about two years with the White Tigers of Ribbons, an armed group operating in the Southwest region, before abandoning the group in 2019.

He was subsequently admitted to a reintegration centre, where he underwent rehabilitation for several years.

He is now a vocal critic of the separatist movement and advocates peace and national unity.

Despite defections and government efforts to encourage fighters to surrender, separatist groups continue to carry out attacks across the two regions.

Some analysts argue that the conflict has persisted for so long partly because of the government's failure to address the grievances that triggered it.

Roland Fru, a researcher on the Anglophone crisis, says the authorities are yet to demonstrate a clear understanding of the issues driving the conflict.

"I've not heard even once, the government explain things in such a way that helps me understand that they even understand the problem," he says.

The BBC contacted several government officials for comment but did not receive a response.

Cameroonian authorities have frequently rejected criticism of their handling of the crisis.

Last November, President Paul Biya said the reconstruction efforts, supported by international partners, were helping restore normality in the affected regions.

"These efforts will continue steadily," he said during his swearing-in ceremony, while praising security forces for their "effective actions" and "professionalism" in improving the situation.

For survivors such as Median, Glory and Maurine, however, lasting peace may remain elusive until the fighting comes to a permanent end.

As they try to rebuild their lives and hold on to hope, Median, who still lives in Cameroon, says she remains grateful to have survived.

"I have defeated death," she says confidently. "I don't think there's anything that can stop me."

Additional reporting by the BBC's Michel Mvondo

#147. Dozens of oil and shipping tankers hacked in recent weeks, per report

U.S. officials suspect Iranian hackers may be behind as many as 50 cyberattacks targeting shipping vessels in recent weeks, according to a report by The Wall Street Journal.

The ships carrying oil, liquefied gas, and other explosive chemicals were hacked as they traveled across Asia and Europe. Several oil tankers headed toward the U.S. coast in August also had their navigation systems breached, prompting cybersecurity teams within the FBI and Coast Guard to board the vessels.

The report said officials are investigating if Iran is expanding its recent wave of hacks against the U.S. by targeting large vessels to sow fear among shippers, disrupt routes, or to cause an explosion at a port. (Ships have backup controls in the event that automated systems are hacked or fail.)

Hackers may have been abusing a bug dubbed FortiBleed, which allows hackers to break into networks by compromising vulnerable Fortinet-made routers. A joint FBI-Coast Guard security alert from September warned that Fortinet routers are used on shipping vessels.

Iran has been firing back on all sides since the start of the U.S./Israel-led war earlier this year, which killed the country’s supreme leader. Many of Iran’s retaliations have been in the form of cyberattacks, with U.S. officials blaming Iran for hacks targeting hundreds of American water providers and U.S. medical tech companies.

A spokesperson for the FBI did not respond to a request for comment about the shipping hacks or whether it had a more accurate figure.

#145. Clashing political visions reflect a divided Spain ahead of election

Pedro SÃ¡nchez has won praise from the left for standing up to Donald Trump and promoting progressive causes

Speaking in a wrestling ring on the island of Tenerife as he campaigned ahead of Sunday's general election, Spain's Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez cautioned those listening about the spread of right-wing governments.

"What good has President [Donald] Trump done the United States and, by extension, the world - was he a change or a step backwards?" he asked supporters, before invoking the administrations of Brazil's former leader, Jair Bolsonaro, and Argentinian President Javier Milei.

"If we don't want that for the world, why would we want it for our own country?" said SÃ¡nchez.

He issued his warning as Spain's conservative People's Party (PP) leads most of the polls and appears poised to form a governing coalition with the hard-right Vox. The election comes amid a deeply polarised political atmosphere in which both left and right have warned about their opponents' ability to undermine democracy.

In a Europe dominated by right-leaning governments, SÃ¡nchez has gained a reputation as the continent's most outspoken and uncompromising left-wing prime minister.

His strident rhetoric defending feminism, immigration and green energy, along with his condemnation of Israel's military action in Gaza and verbal clashes with Trump, has fostered this image.

Having overseen consistent economic growth in recent years, his stock is high among the international left.

Yet in Spain, SÃ¡nchez has become a hate figure for the right, who see him as an autocrat with little regard for the separation of state powers, obsessed with clinging on to power and surrounded by a corrupt cabal.

Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o's right-wing People's Party (PP) is currently ahead in the polls

In a bullring in Valencia, the same day that SÃ¡nchez was staging his rally, PP leader Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o told supporters that the last few years of Socialist-led government had been "insulting and even humiliating for a lot of people". He added: "We know them, they are capable of anything."

A couple of days earlier, Ignacio Garriga, spokesman for Vox, had gone further, describing SÃ¡nchez as "a very dangerous psychopath".

Both the PP and Vox have cast doubt on the neutrality of postal votes and votes cast by Spaniards abroad ahead of the election, suggesting that government manipulation could sway them in its favour.

"This is a campaign that is overwhelmingly ideological," said Cristina Monge, a political scientist at the Complutense University. "That means that it is not so much about voters rewarding or punishing a government's performance or the proposals being made, but about belonging to one or other ideological bloc."

Much of the hostility to SÃ¡nchez stems from his reliance on hard-left and separatist parties.

Ahead of the 2023 general election, he faced fierce criticism from the right for his reliance on the pro-independence EH Bildu coalition, which had links to the defunct Basque armed group ETA.

After that vote, he was able to form a new administration with the support of another separatist party, Together for Catalonia (Junts), in exchange for an amnesty for all Catalans facing legal action for their role in a failed 2017 bid for independence.

At the time, NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o accused SÃ¡nchez of being "a prime minister who has bought his investiture in exchange for the judicial impunity of his partners".

Guillermo FernÃ¡ndez VÃ¡zquez, a political scientist at Carlos III University, said those parliamentary partnerships "fed the rhetoric of the right and far right, which was that Pedro SÃ¡nchez is an amoral person without scruples â someone whose word can't be trusted and who just wants to remain in power".

This legislation has been one of turmoil for SÃ¡nchez, during which he has been unable to push a single budget through parliament.

Shock of rail tragedy gives way to sorrow and outrage in politically divided Spain

In addition, the prime minister has been corralled by a litany of judicial investigations. He has acknowledged errors of judgement in trusting two close former associates in the Socialist Party, JosÃ© Luis Ãbalos, who has been given a 24-year-jail sentence for corruption-related crimes, and Santos CerdÃ¡n, who is due to go on trial on similar charges.

By contrast, he has defended his former attorney general, Ãlvaro GarcÃ­a Ortiz, who was barred from public office for revealing confidential information, and his brother, David, who was barred from office for nine years after being found guilty of irregularities in taking on a post as a regional arts director.

In the most controversial case, SÃ¡nchez's wife, BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez, is due to go on trial for influence peddling.

She denies wrongdoing and SÃ¡nchez has said the cases involving GarcÃ­a Ortiz, his brother and wife are "nasty politics, not corruption", blaming them on efforts by his political opponents to use the judiciary to destabilise his government.

Politicians on the right draw no such distinction between the various cases, and Vox leader Santiago Abascal describes the government as "a criminal mafia".

MarÃ­a PÃ©rez, a young office worker who has no political allegiance, says she is going to vote for the PP, partly because of her concerns about the country's housing crisis and the rail system, which has seen two major tragedies this year, but above all due to the judicial investigations.

"I think that SÃ¡nchez has lost credibility with all the corruption cases," she says. "It seems that everyone in government is involvedâ¦ so I'm not sure I want them to continue governing. I believe it's time for a change."

Santiago Abascal and his far-right Vox party could enter government for the first time after Sunday's election

Allegations that SÃ¡nchez has co-opted state institutions, such as the CIS national research centre, which publishes opinion polls, and national broadcaster RTVE, have only compounded the right's view of him as a populist who has scant regard for the rule of law.

However, with the far right seemingly set to enter government for the first time in Spain's democratic era, claims of demagoguery cut both ways.

Politicians on the left say that coalition administrations formed by the PP and Vox in four of Spain's 17 regions are a blueprint for socially regressive policies they plan to implement on a national level after the election.

In all four of those regions, Vox has negotiated the introduction of "national priority", which seeks to give Spaniards priority access to public services and subsidies ahead of foreigners.

The Castilla y LeÃ³n region has also seen culture-related controversies driven by Vox. Most recently, a play about left-wing poet and dramatist Federico GarcÃ­a Lorca, who was killed by right-wing rebels during the 1936-39 civil war, was cancelled in the region's capital, Valladolid, apparently at the behest of the Vox-controlled local culture ministry.

Last month the same department published new guidelines governing the awarding of subsidies for films, stating that projects involving what it called "ideological indoctrination" would not be eligible. Spain's culture minister, Ernest Urtasun, described this as "an intolerable attempt at censorship".

Susana Fuertes, a schoolteacher who will vote for the left on Sunday, said such moves are a sinister throwback to the dictatorship of Francisco Franco.

"These political messages take us back to the Franco era by talking about getting rid of basic civil rights regarding women, gender equality and immigration," she said. "All of this has been said in such an aggressive tone, with insults and lack of respect for people who don't share the same ideas."

"These political messages take us back to the Franco era," Susana Fuertes says

Critics of NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o argue that he has already shown signs of taking on Vox's agenda by hardening his rhetoric on immigration and promising to roll back a law allowing euthanasia and eliminate the equality ministry, which manages matters related to gender, sexuality and ethnicity.

Spain's deep left-right schism has also been visible beyond the political arena.

Hooded anti-fascist youths have clashed with police on a number of occasions on the campaign trail, such as when they demonstrated against an appearance by Vox's Abascal in A CoruÃ±a.

An arguably more visible phenomenon has been far-right youths. In one incident, several dozen of them raised their arms in the fascist salute and sang the pro-Franco anthem Cara al Sol (Facing the sun) while facing off against housing protesters in Madrid.

A 2025 CIS poll found that around one in five under-25s had a positive view of the dictatorship.

#144. Maple raises $5.3M to build AI products that protect your data, even from itself

“We wanted to build something that was not only encrypted but also could be proven, and that people could go look at the open-source code, and then they could look at what we call trusted execution environments,” explains Maple co-founder and CEO Mark Suman. “They can look at and verify the code that’s running on our servers, so they don’t really have to take our word for it.”

“They can audit everything themselves and know how their data is being handled,” he adds.

Instead of asking users to trust that Maple won’t misuse their personal data, it’s attempting to build a business where it can’t access that data in the first place.

Like other AI apps on the market, Maple allows users to engage in a chat experience where they ask questions and get answers. Meanwhile, its newly launched desktop app, Maple Work, offers a more agentic experience where the AI can control your computer, work with your files and apps, and control your browser to get online tasks handled.

With the company’s recently closed $5.3 million round, it’s moving on to its next product — a cloud-based agent that will be able to continue its work even if your computer isn’t on. This addition would make Maple more competitive with other popular consumer AI services, like Meta’s Muse, Instinct, and other AI agents that work over chat.

The app offers a familiar interface for those used to AI chatbots, but under the hood it runs a variety of open models, including versions of DeepSeek, Kimi, GLM, Inkling from Thinking Machines, Google’s Gemma, and OpenAI’s GPT OSS. Users can pick and choose the models they want or, for a simpler experience, just toggle between a quick model and the more powerful one.

The models themselves run inside a trusted execution environment (TEE) — meaning a server with hardware encryption on it — within a U.S.-based data center. (Maple also offers support for European customers through a partner that runs its server in Europe.)

“We actually don’t have access to [the data] while it’s inside the TEE,” explains Suman. “The data gets encrypted first on your device using a unique password that’s only known by you, the user — we don’t have access to it. It then goes into this TEE, and it gets decrypted inside there. The AI does its thing, and then it re-encrypts it with your password and sends it back to you,” he continues. “So we provide an end-to-end experience that’s very easy for the user, but we actually don’t have access to the raw data as it’s in flight or even when it’s at rest,” he adds.

This allows Maple to protect its users’ data, even from outsiders — like if hackers breached its system or the government came with a subpoena, for instance. All it could do would be to hand over an encrypted file.

“We just simply don’t want access to it,” Suman says of customers’ data. “That’s not part of our business model. And we also don’t want it to be part of our threat model.”

Next year, Maple plans to enter the enterprise market with pilot work, where it will offer its own version of zero data retention. (Maple doesn’t retain most of the customers’ data, but when it has to, that data is encrypted.) It’s now in the process of completing SOC 2 compliance and security audits to get ready for this expansion.

The company declined to share its registered customer numbers but noted it’s seen 60x growth over the past three months, as people have been looking for ways to increase their security with AI.

Maple currently generates revenue via subscriptions, which are comparable to those from other AI model providers. Its Pro plan is $20 per month, and it offers two Max plans, Max and Max Plus, at $100 and $200 per month, respectively.

The Austin- and San Francisco-based startup is a team of four and is actively hiring.

#143. Gukesh to defend chess crown against rising star Sindarov

As the World Chess championship gets underway, it's no coincidence that the titleholder, Gukesh Dommaraju, and his challenger, Jakhovir Sindarov, are both from Asia.

When India's world chess champion, Dommaraju Gukesh, faced reporters at the pre-tournament press conference in Geneva on Sunday, there was no sign of the youthful, carefree energy one might expect from a 20-year-old.

"I don't think I'm here to defend anything—I'm here to win it all over again," he said.

Starting November 24, Gukesh will be facing challenger Jakhovir Sindarov of Uzbekistan for a purse of $2.5 million (€2.3m) in the World Chess Championship.

Gukesh captured the title in 2024, making him the youngest-ever world champion at just 18. Since then, however, things have not as gone well for the player known for his exceptional calculation skills and a penchant for risky moves. He currently languishes in 26th place in the FIDE world rankings.

Sindarov, who has climbed to number four in the world, told reporters that he only began training "seriously" last year and has learned a lot from observing the reigning world champion.

"He has a very good strength in typical positions, his calculation is very good, and Gukesh can fight until the end," Sindarov said.

Former world champion Viswanathan Anand, who was also one of the world's best players as a teenager, is not completely surprised by Gukesh's lack of form over the past two years.

"He became the world champion (very young). So suddenly the responsibility is shifted onto you and even expectations," Anand told DW.

"Clearly he has deficiencies in his chess as is normal for someone who's 20, he's not had time to experience a lot."

By the time he became India's first world champion, Anand was 37 – ancient by the age-standards of today's top players.

"With powerful chess software and global connectivity, it is easier for young players today to reach the top level," Jan Gustafsson, coach of Germany's national chess team, told DW.

"Online chess provides many more learning opportunities for talented players," added Gustafsson, who sees the recent movement at the top of the rankings as a "generational shift."

While Norway's Magnus Carlsen, long considered the greatest player of all time, remains number one in the world rankings, he rarely plays classical chess anymore and gave up his world championship title after declining to defend it in 2023.

Now, young players—particularly from Asia—are pushing to the forefront. Alongside Gukesh and Sindarov, players such as Rameshbabu Praggnanandhaa from India and Nodirbek Abdusattorov of Uzbekistan—an emerging chess powerhouse—are considered contenders to win a world championship in the not-too-distant future.

"Broad social acceptance and state support ensure that children can focus on chess at a very early stage," Gustafsson said.

Another contender is Germany's Vincent Keymer, who at 22 is number nine in the world and well established among the world's elite. Most recently, Keymer and his teammates won a bronze medal at the World Team Championship, often referred to as the "Chess Olympiad."

#142. Lies, a disguise and surfboard bags: how a jealous Sydney policeman tried to cover up double murder

Watch: The CCTV that helped convict a Sydney policeman of double murder

It was just after 09:00 on a warm February morning when CCTV picked up a man dressed as an UberEats rider hovering on a sidewalk in one of Sydney's wealthiest neighbourhoods.

He dawdled for around 20 minutes, "steeling himself for what he was about to do" prosecutors say, before disappearing out of sight. Minutes later another CCTV camera captured the sound of gunshots ringing out.

Former police officer Beau Lamarre, 31, was on Wednesday found guilty of murdering his former partner Jesse Baird, a TV presenter, and Luke Davies, Baird's new partner, with his work-issued gun.

In February 2024, the bloodied belongings of Baird and Davies were found in a skip in the suburb of Cronulla, sparking a frantic search for the couple. It culminated in the discovery of their bodies on a rural property, partially buried beneath sticks and debris.

Luke Davies (left) and Jesse Baird had been dating only months when they were gunned down

During a two-month trial filled with at-times extraordinary testimony and a wealth of CCTV evidence, the court heard Lamarre had planned his crime over months.

Jurors watched CCTV of the then police officer purchasing a surfboard bag in which to put Baird's body, after casually sizing it up against a friend in the shop, and heard from a stream of former friends and colleagues who testified that Lamarre asked them for advice about everything from mobile phone signals to where he could buy a gun.

One recounted the chilling moment Lamarre told her that if he were ever to murder someone, he would leave their belongings in a dumpster and hide the body in bushland where they would never be found.

"What would you do if I told you I killed somebody? Would you help me hide the body?" he asked, according to her testimony.

Later, Lamarre asked for help in covering up the killings; his friends - none of whom have been implicated - say they began receiving panicked calls and odd requests from him.

One told the court Lamarre persuaded her to go for a drive in the country, during which he tried to dispose of the bodies at the family farm of a man he'd met on a dating app. Over the speakers, he played Taylor Swift's No Body, No Crime.

Baird, 26, and Lamarre - a one-time celebrity blogger who also had his own swimwear line on the side of his police work - met in a nightclub in October 2022. For Lamarre, it quickly became an infatuation, but for Baird, an AFL referee and former Network Ten presenter, the relationship appeared to be casual.

After discovering in April 2023 that Lamarre had gone through messages on his phone, and unfollowed other men on his Instagram account, Baird broke it off.

But Lamarre's obsession only grew. He watched Baird having sex with another man through his bedroom window, broke into his house multiple times, deleting contacts on his phone and turning on location settings so that he could engineer meetings with the TV presenter.

On one occasion Baird woke up to find Lamarre in his bedroom; not realising who it was, he chased him down the street. Lamarre was also convicted of aggravated break and enter over that incident on Wednesday.

Prosecutor Brett Hatfield said Baird was "oblivious to the accused's scheming and manipulations", and the pair briefly rekindled the relationship before Baird ended it definitively in November.

He'd discovered Lamarre had, again, been accessing his phone and had attempted to ward off a love rival by pretending to have received flowers from Baird.

Beau Lamarre pictured at the annual Gay and Lesbian Mardi Gras parade in Sydney in 2020

His pride wounded, Hatfield said Lamarre's thoughts turned to a "darker place": "revenge".

He asked an old friend if her ex-partner could get him a gun: "This was the beginning of the plan for the murder," the prosecutor said.

In the months leading up to the killings, Lamarre bought a burner phone, a motorcycle helmet, and practised shooting in the woods. On holiday with a friend in Bali in January 2024 he tried to buy a silencer.

While his murder plans percolated, he also began lashing out with petty acts of malice, prosecutors said, such as putting water in the fuel tank of Baird's car.

But at the same time as he plotted, Lamarre was still trying to win his ex back.

On one occasion he pressured a friend to send Baird a 1,600-word message he had himself composed, claiming that he had cancer, and may need to have his arm amputated.

Under questioning in court, he said a naturopath he couldn't remember the name of had diagnosed him with "a form of sporadic cancer".

By the end of 2023, Baird had met and hit it off with Davies, 29. "It was early days but they were both smitten with each other," his mother, Helen Baird, told the court. He sent her a picture of the pair at a Pink concert. "I just sent a big love heart and said 'I'm so happy for both of you'."

Jesse Baird and Luke Davies at the Pink concert shortly before they were killed

Early on the morning of 19 February, Lamarre picked up a hire car he had asked his mother to rent and drove to Baird's home. After waiting several hours for Baird's housemates to leave, while disguised as an UberEats driver, he let himself in with a key he had copied.

Lamarre told the court he had been at Baird's home to pick up some belongings, including a A$25,000 (US$16,500, Â£12,600) Cartier ring, and had disguised himself to avoid the embarrassment of being recognised if anyone was home.

He claimed he let himself in when no one answered the door, and the two men were killed in separate struggles. He argued that Baird had been holding a knife and the first shot was fired to disarm him, and the second by accident. Davies shot himself when he grabbed the gun, Lamarre said.

But prosecutors told the jury Baird was shot in the back and then once again through his temple as he lay gravely wounded on the kitchen floor.

The evidence, Hatfield said, pointed to one thing only: "an execution".

Davies was then shot through a bedroom door as he tried to call emergency services. The court heard the panic in his voice as the seconds-long call was replayed before it dropped out.

After that, Lamarre set about trying to hide the evidence of what Hatfield called his "brazen and shocking murders".

One of his first actions was to call a friend. He testified Lamarre confessed he had "killed people" and asked him to take a day off work "to help him clean up his mess".

Later Lamarre pretended the call was a joke, saying, "Did you like my Oscar performance? Got you".

Meanwhile, Lamarre went to a DIY store to purchase mops, bleach and bin bags. After returning to Baird's house to clean up, he left the bodies wrapped in surfboard bags and tarpaulins in the courtyard.

Baird had been set to move out that week, and Lamarre used Baird's phone to reassure his housemates he had hired an Airtasker to move his belongings, adding, "I might be flying to Perth tonight".

Baird's housemates quickly spotted the surfboard bags when they returned home, snapping a photograph tendered in court which showed "red liquid" seeping out.

"I thought it was rusted water from the outdoor furniture," one of the women said.

The surfboard bags were gone the next morning.

Lamarre was captured on CCTV sizing up a surfboard bag in the days before the murders

Lamarre had hired a van, and over the next two days made more trips to the DIY store and a sports shop to buy gumboots, a wheelbarrow, bigger surfboard bags and 10kg weights. He squeezed in a hospital appointment for a minor procedure and paused for a sushi date with a friend, CCTV showing him laughing and eating as the bodies lay in the back of his van.

On 21 February, he picked up another friend in the van for what she thought was lunch.

There were blue tarps in the back and it smelled "lemony", she told the court. She "wasn't allowed to put the windows up or turn the aircon off" as they motored towards Goulburn, 200km southwest of Sydney, stopping only at another DIY store, where he asked her to buy an angle grinder.

His friend told the court she remembers Lamarre playing No Body, No Crime at some point: "It's a very niche song."

Arriving at a farm owned by a Hinge date's family, Lamarre used the angle grinder to cut through a padlock, making his way to a dam where he unsuccessfully tried to sink the bodies.

It was now late at night and he drove his friend, by this time upset and confused, home before returning to retrieve the bodies. He then left them in the van, parked in Sydney overnight, before driving once more to Goulburn, where he dumped the bodies near a monastery.

Later that day he visited another friend, telling her to look up the definition of schizophrenia and asking whether he could use her hose, because "there was a bit of blood he had to scrub off" the hired van, the court heard.

The deaths of Baird and Davies triggered an outpouring of horror in Sydney

By this time police were closing in, and on 23 February Lamarre went to turn himself in at Bondi police station. On the way, he called a police helpline.

"I've been bullied by multiple New South Wales police force members," he told the woman on the other end of the line. "All this stress and trauma and psychological abuse and trauma - did I say trauma already? - has caused me to not sleep, get anxious, it's caused me to have some symptoms of schizophrenia."

"Actually sorry, you know what, can you just... delete that whole thing. We'll just keep it short and sweet."

He wasn't sure when he'd be back to work, he said. Shortly afterwards he was arrested and charged with murder, and the bodies of Baird and Davies were found four days later.

Lamarre's lawyer John Stratton insisted he'd acted in self-defence and had "panicked" realising how the scene would look to outsiders.

The former police officer did plenty of "foolish" and "unusual" things - "He was, it's fair to say, a poorly behaved boyfriend," Stratton said - but that did not make him a murderer.

Ultimately, however, the jury was not convinced and took just over an hour to convict him. He will be sentenced in March and faces life in prison.

#140. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#138. Walmart teams up with Meta to bring Black Friday deals to AI agent Muse

Shopping with AI is becoming less of a futuristic idea and more of something companies expect us to do. Walmart is the latest retailer making that shift, teaming up with Meta to let customers shop its Black Friday deals through Muse, Meta’s AI agent.

The partnership, announced Tuesday, gives shoppers another way to find products, compare prices, and make purchases without browsing Walmart’s website themselves. Instead, customers can simply tell Muse what they are looking for and let the AI do the work.

The announcement highlights how differently Walmart and Amazon are approaching AI shopping. While Amazon has taken steps to stop third-party AI agents from making purchases for customers, Walmart is leaning into the idea.

To get started, users open the Muse app, sign in to their Walmart account, and return to the assistant to begin shopping. They can ask it to find specific products, compare options, or track down deals. Once a customer makes a request in Muse, the assistant sends it to Walmart, which returns relevant products and helps build a shopping cart.

Plus, the company notes users can always delete their agent’s profile if they want to.

The experience is powered by Walmart’s Agent Gateway, a new system that allows outside AI assistants to connect directly with the retailer’s online store. This launched in October, and the company is planning to open it up to more developers and AI platforms, meaning Muse could eventually be just one of several assistants capable of shopping Walmart’s online store.

Walmart is not the only company teaming up with Meta’s Muse. Instacart also recently joined forces with the AI assistant.

Beyond Muse, Walmart has recently rolled out more AI-powered features to its own shopping assistant, Sparky.

Some of the newer additions include turning handwritten shopping lists into shopping carts, using photos and voice commands to find products, and helping customers get around their local store. Shoppers can search for an item and use a pin on the store map to see exactly which aisle it’s in. There’s also a “Flash Price Tag” button that makes the item’s shelf label light up.

#137. US and UK to host Women's World Cups in 2031 and 2035

FIFA has confirmed the United States, Mexico, Costa Rica and Jamaica will host the 2031 Women's World Cup — the first to feature 48 teams. The UK will stage the 2035 edition, its first World Cup since 1966.

FIFA have confirmed the hosts for the 2031 and 2035 Women's World Cup.

The United States, along with Mexico, Costa Rica and Jamaica, made an uncontested bid to host in 2031. It will be the first tournament to feature 48 teams — as is the case with the men. The US has hosted the event twice before, in 2003 and 1999, and is also the most successful country in its history, having won it four times.

FIFA's revenue for the 2026 men's World Cup was around $15 billion (€13.7 billion), and it's clear that US Soccer feels that hosting the women's tournament is the next move forward. With the women's domestic league (NWSL) considered the strongest in the world and the US national team so dominant, securing the tournament in 2031 is a further sign of the US' committment to women's football.

"We expect and plan for the 2031 Women’s World Cup to be the largest women’s sporting event in history," Emily Cosler, US Soccer’s vice-president of strategy told The Guardian.

The 2035 tournament was also awarded to the sole bidders, England, Scotland, Northern Ireland and Wales. This sees the World Cup return to the United Kingdom for the first time since 1966, the only time England's men's team won the competition. The women lost the 2023 final to Spain. It also means that the UK will host two major tournaments in relatively quick succession, with the men's 2028 European Championships also set to be held in England, Scotland and Wales and the Republic of Ireland.

British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the tournament would bring "visitors, investment and jobs to every part of the country."

Given that FIFA President Gianni Infantino had revealed last year that both bids were unchallenged, this news comes as little surprise. Nevertheless, with the hosts now official, a 48-team tournament marks a remarkable advance for the women's game. The 2011 World Cup in Germany had just 16 teams.

The next World Cup will be in Brazil in 2027, running from June 24 to July 25. . Eighteen of the 32 teams have already secured qualification, including Germany.

#136. My family died in Ukraine. My mum blamed Zelensky's troops - the truth is complicated

Watch: How the Russia-Ukraine war tore my family apart

I am sitting on my balcony in Kyiv looking at a photo of a garden in the village I grew up in, in eastern Ukraine. The plot is overgrown with thick grass, and two wooden stakes are planted in the ground. Despite the warm sunshine, I find myself shivering.

The woman who sent me the photo says the stakes mark the graves of my grandparents, killed as the Ukrainian-Russian front line reached our little community. Not far away, in another garden in the village, the body of my father, Serezha, is buried in a greenhouse.

The photo Anastasiya was sent of her grandparents' graves

I cover the Russia-Ukraine war for the BBC World Service, but found myself in the middle of that story in February last year when my mother sent me a devastating text message.

"Granny, grandad and Serezha are gone. Now there is no-one left," it said.

Because not only had I lost three beloved family members, my mother blamed Ukraine for their deaths - the country where I was born and have lived for most of my life.

Mum was born in Siberia. From the moment Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, she had supported Russia in the conflict.

Anastasiya (bottom left) with her father (top left) and grandparents (right) before the war

Backing Moscow is not that unusual in the Donbas region - the predominantly Russian-speaking part of eastern Ukraine where my village Dachne is located.

After pro-Russian forces captured large parts of the Donbas in 2014, many of those who stayed supported Moscow over Kyiv, a stance cemented by exposure to Russian state TV.

Now the Russians occupy almost 80% of Donetsk - the part of the Donbas where my village is. Over the past nearly five years, since Russia's full-scale invasion, entire towns and cities have been razed to the ground, external.

The remains of Anastasiya's family home are circled in a frame of a video, filmed by Ukraine's 33rd Mechanised Brigade on 27 January 2025

I had already fallen out with my sister Sasha, who had been living in Moscow with her young family when the war broke out. She refused to take sides - I felt hurt and cut her off.

But earlier this year, around the first anniversary of Dad's death, Sasha unexpectedly texted me. We started talking over text about our childhood - the way things used to be. I realised I was not angry with her anymore. She too was grieving, missing Dad just as much as I was.

Anastasiya's father Serezha - he stayed in Dachne to look after his elderly parents

I told her I wanted to make a documentary about Dad and she was very supportive. I knew then that I had an ally on my journey. But we decided not to tell our mum just yet.

I started looking for witnesses to the events in Dachne that took place in January 2025, the month Dad is believed to have been killed. It was not easy. It was a village of a few hundred people, but many had left, others had died, and those who remained had limited phone signals.

Russia publicly announced the capture of Dachne on 14 February and it has remained under Moscow's control ever since.

This meant it was impossible for me to travel there in person. So I began trying to contact my family's neighbours.

Anastasiya could not safely travel back to Dachne, so she worked through contacts in village group chats

In a Dachne village messaging chat, I found the number of a man I'll call Misha. I was told he had been with my father on the day he died. Speaking to the BBC could put him in danger with the Russian occupiers, so I did not tell him I worked for the BBC or that I was recording our call.

Misha told me that on 26 January he had looked in on my father and realised he had been injured. Serezha told him he had been "clipped" by a drone, and had asked Misha to bring a first aid kit, he said.

It is impossible to know which side was operating the drone that killed my father, but we do know that Russian drones were constantly targeting the village at that time.

"I asked if the wound was serious. He [Serezha] said it wasn't," said Misha - but then recalling how, when he had returned to check on my father a couple of hours later, he had found him dead.

With the help of two other villagers, Misha said they had buried him in the greenhouse.

"I'm sorry I couldn't save him," Misha told me.

BBC Eye Investigations: Who Killed My Family?

BBC journalist Anastasiya Gribanova investigates who killed her father and grandparents in Ukraine, uncovering painful accounts as she tries to reunite her divided family.

Watch on BBC iPlayer or listen on BBC Sounds now (UK only)

If you are outside of the UK, listen to the Who killed my family podcast here

As for my grandparents, my investigations suggested the events surrounding their deaths may have been more complicated.

Their house, on the eastern side of Dachne, had been destroyed. Neighbours told me they had escaped to another home on the same street.

I eventually reached a woman I'll call Lina, who lived nearby and was the last person, as far as I know, who saw my grandparents alive.

She could not recall what this date was, but knew it was in the second half of January.

At this time, she said, "the Russians were fully advancing - the edges of the village were being shelled. Then the Ukrainian soldiers moved from the edge of the village into the centre. They hid in people's yards and used side streets to avoid drones."

She said two soldiers entered her property. They had not been wearing any identifying insignia but had been speaking Ukrainian, she said - a language that Lina described as "Bandera", a derogatory term for Ukrainians.

"You're going to have to leave. Why are you still here?" Lina said they had asked her.

She said one had pointed a machine gun at her as she had stood outside her house, while another had gone to see my grandparents. She told me she had feared for her life and then fled her house for the night.

The following day, she told me she had returned to my grandparents' street to check on them.

She had found the door to the house they had been staying in open, she said, despite the freezing conditions.

With drones buzzing overhead, Lina said she had not dared look inside, but had later heard from neighbours that my grandparents had been found dead. She believed it had been the Ukrainian soldiers who visited her house who had shot them.

Another neighbour, who I will call Maria, described discovering their bodies - my grandmother in bed, covered in a blanket, and my grandfather lying on the floor in a pool of now-dried blood.

Maria also believed Ukrainian soldiers had killed them - because, she said, that she too had felt threatened by Ukrainian troops at the time.

"If they had seen me they would have killed me too."

Her theory was that my grandparents had irritated the troops in some way.

"Your grandparents caught them in a bad mood because your grandfather was always asking to be evacuated. And [the soldiers] were, you know, high. They were taking drugs. So your grandparents got in their way," she told me.

Most of the other people in the village I spoke to also believe Ukrainian soldiers killed them, though their testimony is not based on direct evidence.

The new local mayor, appointed by the Russian occupiers when they finally took control of Dachne in February, said she had been present at my grandparents' burial the following month, and said half of my grandmother's skull was missing.

These accounts of how they might have died in January 2025 were deeply disturbing, though no-one had witnessed their deaths and the information I was given could not be independently verified.

The villagers I spoke to - now living in occupied territory - did say there had been some tensions between themselves and Ukrainian troops at the time, because the soldiers had viewed those who remained in Dachne as supporters of Russia.

The clinical causes of my grandparents' deaths are unclear.

Their bodies were not exhumed for nearly 20 months, when the local authorities sent them to a morgue in the occupied region, where a basic autopsy was carried out by a forensics expert.

He told me that my grandmother had died of an explosives injury to the skull - but could not specify its cause.

As for my grandfather, it said his body had shown no obvious signs of injury.

I compared two satellite images of the house in which their bodies were found - from just before and just after when they are believed to have died - and could detect no significant damage to the building in the second image.

I also spoke to a villager who said he had buried my grandparents.

He said he did not know anything about who killed them.

"I wasn't there. But why would you kill old people?" he asked.

Anastasiya does not know if she will ever be able to give her grandparents (pictured) a dignified burial

I examined military maps from DeepStateMap.Live, an open-source Ukrainian resource, and US think tank Institute for the Study of War. They suggested Ukrainian forces still held the street where my grandparents lived during the week they had died, although the front line had been shifting rapidly.

My colleagues helped me identify the Russian military units that went on to capture Dachne. We found telephone numbers of Russian soldiers. But none of them agreed to speak to me.

A soldier from the 33rd Mechanised Brigade, who gave his name simply as Vlad, said his unit had still been in Dachne at the time my grandparents were believed to have died and that the Russians had been swiftly advancing.

Another Ukrainian soldier I spoke to also confirmed Ukraine's troops had still been in control of some parts of Dachne in late January.

We put our findings on the deaths of my grandparents to the Ukrainian military.

It said it had no verified evidence on how they died, or which implicated its servicemen during the "intense hostilities". It added that investigating allegations of wrongdoing was the role of law enforcement.

We also approached Russia's Ministry of Defence and Russia's embassy in London for comment - they did not respond.

Sisters Sasha (right) and Anastasiya were reunited at Belgrade Airport

The Donetsk regional prosecutor's office, which is now based outside Russian-controlled territory, has documented alleged Russian war crimes reported by eyewitnesses in villages around Dachne during December 2024 and January 2025 - including the killing of five civilians in a basement in the nearby village of Kostyantynopilske.

However, Ukrainian investigators have no equivalent cases registered in Dachne during that period. They have documented only three deaths there. My father and grandparents are not among them.

Their bodies lie in occupied territory, and I do not know if I will ever be able to say goodbye properly and bury them with dignity.

There is little more I can do for them, except one thing - mend my relationship with my sister and my mother.

Almost three years after I last saw my sister, I fly to Belgrade where she now lives.

Anastasiya (left) and her sister Sasha look through photos of their father

She is waiting for me at the airport, holding lilies, and a plate with a silly nickname on it that she gave me when we were children. We hug, cry and laugh. It feels as if something between us is finally falling back into place

At her flat, we look through family photographs and talk about my investigation and what I was able to find. My grief is still there, but it hurts a little less. I'm no longer carrying it alone.

"Are you ready?" Sasha asks. She rings our mother - who has also moved to Serbia - and puts the phone on speaker.

"Hi, Mum. It's Nastya," I say. First there is silence. Then I hear her crying. "Nastya, my darling, hello," she replies.

#135. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#134. Weapons, war and wavering trust: Germans want to see more restraint in foreign policy

According to a study by the Körber Foundation, a growing number of Germans now oppose providing military aid to Ukraine. The majority also believes that Germany should take a neutral position between the US and China.

For decades, German foreign policy was never seen as particularly controversial at home. The country was firmly integrated into NATO and supported EU expansion and cooperation across Europe. It also advocated for multilateral approaches to resolving conflicts around the world. All of this enjoyed broad public support. But all of this has also changed since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine and the wars in the Middle East. Now, Germany's foreign policy has become increasingly contentious and is fueling growing public anxiety.

Long-held assumptions are beginning to crumble, including the belief that the US would ultimately remain firmly on Germany's side. This is one of the central findings of a new survey by the Hamburg-based Körber Foundation.

In the foundation's latest poll on public attitudes on German foreign policy, 65% of respondents said they fear further military conflicts around the world. Meanwhile, 48% believe that Germany should adopt a more restrained approach to international affairs — a five percentage point increase from last year.

Since 2017, the non-profit Körber Foundation, established in the northern German city of Hamburg in 1959 by industrialist Kurt Körber, has conducted the annual surveys titled "the Berlin Pulse." For this year's study, the polling institute Forsa surveyed around 1,500 eligible voters in September.

The findings make one thing clear: The current government's approach under Chancellor Friedrich Merz — seeking to have a say in virtually every international conflict and drawing on Germany's position as the largest EU economy — is increasingly met with skepticism. Only 40% of respondents now favor greater international engagement, down from 48% in 2025.

Florian Bigge, who helped compile the study for the Körber Foundation, commented on the findings on Monday: "This is a significant decline, especially since we have been asking this question for years, with changes in the range of just two to three percentage points."

Meanwhile, 77% of respondents want Germany to focus primarily on diplomacy rather than military action.

The most dramatic shift in public opinion concerns the war in Ukraine. Germany's military support for the Eastern European country, including the delivery of billions of euros' worth of weapons, no longer has the backing of a majority of the population. In September 2026, only 46% of respondents supported continued military assistance, compared to 59% in the previous year's survey. Currently, a narrow majority of 53% want to halt weapons deliveries altogether.

As Jonathan Lehrer of "the Berlin Pulse" explains, opposition is particularly strong among supporters of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD).

"Eighty-eight precent of AfD supporters oppose military assistance to Ukraine," Lehrer said. "This is an interesting finding, especially considering political developments in Germany, including state elections in which the AfD gained support by campaigning against aid to Ukraine."

The AfD has recently overtaken the chancellor's conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU) in nationwide polls.

Support for Ukraine was also the subject of a recent poll conducted by the opinion research institute Infratest Dimap. This study also found that public support for Ukraine is continuing to decline, with a majority now opposed to providing assistance (see graphic).

One finding from the Berlin Pulse study is particularly alarming for politicians and experts committed to transatlantic relations. As the US and China are increasingly seen as rivals on the global stage, 83% of respondents believe that Germany should remain neutral. Only 15% would side with the US.

This may stem from the policies of US President Donald Trump. Florian Bigge of the Körber Foundation said of the widespread preference for neutrality: "This sentiment is strong among Germans across all age groups and political parties. In eastern Germany, support for neutrality is at 92%, compared to 81% in the west. We interpret this as a desire for security but also reluctant commitment: Germans do not want to take sides in this conflict between the great powers."

The picture becomes clearer when respondents are asked about Russia. Under President Vladimir Putin, 82% of those surveyed see Russia as a military threat. However, public opinion is more divided when Germany responds firmly to such threats, including hybrid attacks. For instance, after the attempted drone attack on Leipzig Airport in early May, Germany responded by closing the Russian consulate in Bonn. However, only 51% support such measures, while 46% favor a more restrained approach.

"The gap between external expectations and domestic support is the central challenge facing German foreign policy," said Sarah Pagung of the Körber Foundation.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

One issue that appears to cause relatively little concern among Germans is the conflict in the Middle East, despite the devastating situation in the Gaza Strip following Hamas's terrorist attack on Israel more than three years ago.

This may also have to do with changing public attitudes toward Israel. One question in the study asked: "Does Germany have a special historical and moral responsibility for Israel's security because of the Holocaust?"

The results were striking: 61% of respondents said no, while just 36% answered yes. Among those under 25, opposition to Germany's special responsibility toward the Jewish state was even higher, at 75%.

#132. What happens if a nuclear weapon explodes in space?

Earth's atmosphere offers protection from a nuclear blast in space, but satellites are more exposed. We explain how radiation could damage them, why the effects can linger and what protection is possible.

A nuclear explosion in space would be unlikely to cause widespread injuries on Earth. But it could disable satellites that provide services we depend on every day, including some that save lives.

In the United States, satellite failures could make it harder for emergency crews to communicate in remote areas. If GPS were also disrupted, drivers and pilots could lose navigation guidance, and financial networks could lose a source of precise time.

We have some idea of what would happen because it's already been done.

On the evening of July 8, 1962, people in Hawaii held "watch-the-bomb parties" and followed the countdown on shortwave radio. Then the night sky lit up. The United States had detonated a nuclear weapon about 400 kilometers (250 miles) above the Pacific. The test was called Starfish Prime.

An electromagnetic pulse knocked out some streetlights in Hawaii, and some Pacific radio links briefly went down. The glow faded within minutes, but some satellites failed months later.

The air was too thin for a conventional fireball to form or for a destructive shock wave to reach the ground. Charged particles made denser air below glow, creating an artificial aurora. The atmosphere also absorbed much of the initial radiation.

Satellites are especially vulnerable because they orbit above most of the atmosphere that shields people on the ground from the initial radiation.

In orbit, the initial burst of X-rays can damage spacecraft right away. But Earth's magnetic field can also trap electrons released after the blast, as it did after Starfish Prime. Satellites receive another dose each time they cross the resulting radiation belt. Over time, that exposure can damage electronics and solar panels.

That would depend on where the explosion happened and which satellites crossed the radiation belt. GPS satellites send signals that help phones, planes and ships work out where they are. They orbit about 20,200 kilometers above Earth and could keep working even if satellites closer to Earth failed.

But satellites do different jobs. A ship's emergency beacon can use GPS to work out its location, then send it to rescuers through a separate satellite network. If part of that network failed, the alert could take longer to get through.

The 1967 Outer Space Treaty bars its parties from placing nuclear weapons in orbit. The 1963 Limited Test Ban Treaty prohibits its parties from carrying out nuclear explosions in space.

The Outer Space Treaty has no dedicated system for checking what spacecraft carry. A detonation is easier to detect because US satellite sensors are designed to pick up the signals it gives off.

Shielding could help protect satellite electronics, and backups could keep some services running. During a GPS outage, pilots over the contiguous US can use ground-based navigation aids. Operators could launch replacements, but those satellites could still face lingering radiation or debris.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Radiation could leave many satellites intact but unable to steer. If two objects collided, fragments from the crash could hit other satellites, creating more debris and triggering further collisions. If enough debris built up for the chain reaction to keep going on its own, it could continue even if no new satellites were launched. This is known as Kessler syndrome.

Whether an explosion would trigger such a cascade is uncertain. If it did, the debris could endanger replacement satellites for years.

Some radioactive particles could enter the atmosphere and eventually settle on the ground. But a blast in space is too far away to sweep up the soil and water that carry fallout from a surface explosion.

As the particles disperse, their radioactivity decreases over time. The concentrated local fallout that can expose people near a ground-level blast to high doses would be unlikely.

#131. Even the best AI agents struggle with everyday life

AI companies have spent the last couple of months marketing personal AI agents as tools to take over the digital drudgery of human existence — and millions have been sold on the vision, handing over their shopping schedules, travel plans, and everyday responsibilities to AI assistants. But new research suggests that even the most advanced AI models struggle to reliably and safely navigate the messy details of people’s lives.

According to ShellBench, a new benchmark for everyday agent work, even the most advanced models from OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta failed more than a third of the time. Across all models tested, 15.7% of attempts failed at least one safety requirement, often leading to unintended consequences.

ShellBench was developed in collaboration with researchers at The OpenClaw Foundation, Parsewave, Hugging Face, Red Hat, and UC Berkeley, drawing on more than 10 million anonymized, real-world AI agent interactions to see how agents perform on the everyday work people actually delegate to them.

The research comes as personal AI assistants are finding new popularity among developers, with Meta, OpenAI, and Anthropic all fighting for a piece of the market with Muse, Dots, and Claude Duet, respectively.

In October, Meta said more than 10 million people in the U.S. and Canada had set up its personal AI agent, Muse, within six weeks of launch, with about a quarter using it daily. That number is expected to grow as Meta implants Muse directly into Instagram DMs. Despite growing popularity among consumers, we still have little evidence about how reliable AI personal assistant products actually are.

ShellBench suggests that the unreliable nature of most everyday data sources still causes problems for many state-of-the-art systems. A successful agent should be able to navigate messy personal workspaces containing emails, calendar entries, spreadsheets, receipts, chat logs, outdated information, and phishing attempts, and figure out which information is relevant, what is private, and how to deliver the requested outcome without creating new problems.

It seems many of today’s AI agents are still not up to the task. Not only did even the highest-performing agents frequently fail to meet the requirements of a task, but they also lacked consistency. When researchers ran each task three times under identical conditions, OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra, the highest-scoring model, nailed all three attempts less than half the time.

For autonomous assistants that are meant to run in the background without supervision, it’s a problem if users need to run an agent multiple times to get a result they trust.

Additionally, about 10% of otherwise correct attempts failed at least one safety criterion. Across the models and harnesses tested, agents failed to, for example, adequately account for a child’s school pickup 79.5% of the time. When handling laptop custody records, agents failed a requirement to identify and withhold a sensitive bank upload token in 18.6% of attempts. In another task, agents recommended duplicate or incorrect invoice payments 40.3% of the time.

The findings highlight a major issue for users adopting consumer personal AI assistants, namely that there’s always a risk that an agent can create more headaches in the course of trying to fulfill an otherwise ordinary request.

While AI benchmarks have largely focused on coding, professional work, or testing on specific tasks, ShellBench is attempting to evaluate how models perform on multi-step personal tasks within different agent harnesses — the software that controls how a model accesses information, uses tools, and takes actions.

While the researchers found model choice had a greater overall impact on performance than harness choice, among the more capable models, AI agent performance became more dependent on the harness.

For example, OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra outperformed Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5.2 when operating within the Pi harness, but it performed worse when both models were tested within Claude Code.

Meta’s Muse Wonder Full scored 56% in Pi, but only 38.1% in Claude Code. Meta’s Muse Wonder 1.0, meanwhile, averaged a success rate of 39.7% across the five harnesses tested.

Notably, while the researchers tested models from Meta, OpenAI, and Anthropic, they didn’t evaluate them within the native harnesses of Muse, Dots, or Claude Duet, so the results may not be representative of the experience millions of consumers are having with the product today.

TechCrunch has reached out to Meta, OpenAI, Anthropic, and the researchers in the study.
