#130. Trump's rescue of Nvidia's Huang brings political risks

Trump boosts Nvidia boss return but are US politicians on board?

After the AI chipmaker Nvidia announced it was bringing back embattled former head Jensen Huang and the company's stock shot up like a bullet, President Donald Trump took a very public victory lap.

"I made the deal," he said during a lengthy phone interview with cable business news channel CNBC.

Trump followed that with an AI-generated image posted to his social media platform that riffed on the famous photograph taken moments after the 2024 attempt on his life in Pennsylvania. But instead of Trump in the image, Huang was the one surrounded by Secret Service guards. It depicted him in front of an Nvidia banner - with a raised first clenching a red bra.

The post was classic Trump â potentially offensive, yet still a bit opaque.

Was Trump suggesting Huang survived a scandal â one that includes allegations of recklessly jeopardising US national security by consorting with a suspected Chinese spy â the way he had survived an assassin's bullet?

Was the bra a celebration Huang's reported extramarital affairs? By replacing the US flag with an Nvidia standard, was he claiming that America and one of its largest, most powerful companies are one and the same?

At the very least, the image underscores how inextricably linked Huang and Trump now are, with the fate of the former hinging on the successes and failures of the latter.

And with that comes significant risks for the American president and for Republicans, including Vice-President JD Vance, who played a key part in his efforts to rescue Huang.

While Trump has said that a "very thorough investigation" by federal officials "exonerated" Huang and his reinstatement poses no national security threat, that is far from the final word on the matter.

"Every time you have a Chinese woman that's good looking or beautiful, they say she's a spy," Trump told CNBC, adding that "with Jensen, I don't believe that's true".

Democrats and some concerned Republicans in Congress have a different view.

They are promising to launch their own oversight of Huang and Nvidia, with subpoenas and compelled witness testimony, in order to assess whether they violated laws by allowing China to access sophisticated microchip technology.

State governments could also launch their own investigations.

Any new revelations could further damage the company's standing â and, by association, Trump himself.

"I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access," Republican Senator Tom Cotton posted on X.

"Congress has much to investigate," he said.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

What Jensen Huang's return teaches China's Xi about Trump

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

While Huang has a long record of success at Nvidia, there is no guarantee that he can recapture past glory now that his star his dimmed.

Even if he is free from any criminal liability, the tech mogul is about to be in a much harsher spotlight than he has ever experienced.

Trump also appears to be on the wrong side of public opinion when it comes to AI writ large.

In the recent US midterm elections, candidates on both the left and right campaigned on imposing new restrictions on data centre construction, with many calling for new legal guardrails to prevent AI-fuelled economic and social disruption.

"Rather than addressing the American people's growing concerns about AI, this administration remains asleep at the wheel or willfully ignorant as our most advanced technologies fall into the hands of our greatest competitors," congresswoman Sydney Kamlager-Dove, a California Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told the BBC.

The president's own political base is also suspicious of the new technology, with rural conservatives often rebelling at the demands that big tech companies have placed on their local resources and civic infrastructure.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

The Nvidia story has also exposed rifts in Trump's own administration, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, a China hawk, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, a vocal proponent of AI, publicly feuding over whether to impose new restrictions on AI chip exports.

Both Bessent and Lutnick travelled with Trump on his recent trip to China, although it was the commerce secretary who hitched a ride back to the White House on the Marine One helicopter when Trump returned to Washington last Thursday.

Polling also indicated that political corruption was a top issue for voters this year.

The president's willingness to intervene in the deliberations of a major private company in which he owns millions of dollars of stock could further fuel those concerns.

Trump's decision to help save Huang â noting on Monday that "we have to protect our geniuses, and he certainly is a genius" â could further inflame the public's sense that the rich and powerful, genius or not, play by a different set of rules.

"It almost feels like they've bent the rule, the regulation, to allow this to happen because it's such an important part of our economy right now," Steve Bannon, a former top Trump adviser, said on his podcast on Monday.

"Even in the crash of 2008... I never saw the government step in and take over a board of directors."

For this American president, however, these risks appear to be worth taking because of the stakes involved.

Trump has long viewed the success of the US economy as a key part of his political legacy.

He boasted of the historic growth and low unemployment during his first presidential term (while glossing over the disruptions caused by the Covid pandemic).

And since returning to office, he has celebrated as major US stock indices posted new record highs.

Recent US economic expansion has been fuelled largely by investment in AI technology, and Trump has become one of the industry's biggest boosters.

He scolded states and local governments for blocking data centre construction and predicted that the nation that wins the race for supremacy in AI â which he has tried to rebrand as "super intelligence" â will control the fate of the world.

If Trump views the economy as the key to his political success and AI as instrumental to the health of the US economy, then his dramatic, precedent-shattering actions to try to rescue Nvidia, the company at the centre of America's AI industry, make more sense.

In the weeks since concerns over Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy first surfaced, Nvidia's stock price tumbled more than 32%.

US stock indices tumbled as well, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq down 10% last week and the larger S&P 500 dropping 8.6%.

The recent financial turmoil may have given Trump a glimpse into a future where AI growth is just the latest financial bubble â like the housing market in 2008 and internet companies in 2001 â that is due for a resounding pop, with similarly dire economic consequences to follow.

Trump's intervention on behalf of Huang arrested that drop, at least for a day.

On Tuesday, Nvidia's stock price gave back much of the bounce it had enjoyed, and larger stock indices were mixed. The fate of the America economy and the AI juggernaut that has powered it is still undetermined.

Trump's intervention may not be a crisis fully averted, but only a crisis delayed.

And if an AI reckoning does come, the US president will be hard-pressed to avoid being caught in the maelstrom.

#128. In all-hands meeting, Jensen Huang thanks White House and says he’s in therapy

After a whirlwind period during which Jensen Huang’s life fell apart and then came back together again, the Nvidia founder now wants his staff to know that he’s a changed man.

Huang held Nvidia’s first all-hands meeting on Tuesday since his reinstatement on Monday. During that meeting, CNBC reported that Huang struck a “remorseful tone” and told the crowd “I hurt all of you.”

The founder, whose extramarital affairs have been accused of jeopardizing everything from the stock market to U.S. national security, went on to describe how difficult the current moment is for him: “This is going to be the hardest company meeting I’ve ever done, and the second hardest meeting I have ever done,” Huang reportedly told staff.

“The hardest meeting I’ve ever done was with my wife,” he added.

The CEO admitted in the meeting that he had flouted Nvidia’s code of conduct, which he himself had largely devised. Huang emphasized that the code of conduct was still in effect, but that mitigating circumstances would matter a lot. As Wired reported Huang’s statement, it “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door.”

Huang told staff that he’s in therapy and also said that he was grateful for a second chance at the company.

The CEO is now walking a difficult tight rope between reasserting his authority over one of the world’s most powerful and influential companies, and conceding that he may have made some mistakes. He also tackled the difficult question of President Trump’s role in his restoration. The president reportedly called Nvidia’s board directly to lobby for Huang’s return, but in his talk, Huang downplayed Trump’s role

Huang thanked the administration on Tuesday for “providing Nvidia with quick access to intelligence that he said helped the board determine that the national security risks the company faced in reinstating him were low,” The Wall Street Journal writes.

Huang will likely need the administration’s support as he weathers an upcoming wave of congressional scrutiny from a newly Democrat-led government. The CEO is scheduled to appear for a public Senate Banking Committee hearing next week and, in the coming weeks, he will be called to appear for a number of different closed House hearings, including for the Intelligence Committee, the Foreign Affairs Committee, and the China Select Committee.

#127. German directors' doc on stolen film wins International Emmy Award

Two German film students confronted the American who won festival prizes with their animated movie. "The Talented Mr. F," their documentary on their fight against plagiarism, is now in the spotlight.

It sounds like the plot of a screenplay, but it happened to two Berlin film students in real life. The documentary they made out of their unusual story, "The Talented Mr. F," has now won an International Emmy Award.

Moritz Henneberg and Julius Drost had created an animated short film for their final project at university.

"Butty" tells the tale of a household robot that gets kicked out when it can't do its job properly.

When the two filmmakers posted the work on YouTube, this touching tale quickly went viral. After generating a huge amount of hits, the duo decided to submit their work to festivals — but they were in for a shock: Organizers told them the film had already been submitted under a different name.

US student Samuel Felinton downloaded the film, made minimal edits, gave it a new title, "T-130," and removed the original credits. As the alleged author, he went on to win numerous prizes and made a name for himself in the United States.

Shocked by the blatant theft of their work, Henneberg and Drost sought legal advice. They were told that if they opted to sue, the process would be arduous and costly. So, the filmmakers took matters into their own hands and went to the US to confront Felinton — filming their journey along the way.

"We'd already made documentary films," Henneberg told DW. "And the idea just popped into our heads: This is a brilliant story — let's make a documentary about it."

The two directors had developed a kind of fascination for the man who'd stolen their film.

"We looked at his vlogs and sort of took a deep dive into his world," said Drost. "We almost saw him as a kind of celebrity. We discovered a lot about him and really wanted to meet him in person."

While family and friends reacted with anger, the two young filmmakers remained surprisingly calm. They were more perplexed than anything: "Our families and friends hated Samuel. But we just wanted to understand what had happened," they said.

The pair put together a film crew and traveled to Morgantown, West Virginia — the university town where Felinton lives. With the help of a New York filmmaker pretending to make a documentary about young animators, they won Felinton's trust — and then Henneberg, Drost and Felinton were finally in a room together.

The two German filmmakers had prepared for all manner of scenarios ahead of the meeting: "We expected an emotional reaction — that he'd run away, cry, be ashamed or get aggressive. But he remained completely calm. That was the only thing we hadn't bargained for. We thought his world would implode. But he showed barely any emotion at all," they recalled.

Felinton explained how he'd shortened and "improved" the film and that this was how it became such a success. He agreed to transfer any prize money. His manner was cool and matter-of-fact, which Drost and Henneberg found both unsettling and fascinating. After their chat, the three barbecued together and played a round of basketball.

Later, many viewers accused the two German filmmakers of being too soft on the plagiarist. Others praised their approach.

"The reactions were very divided," said Drost. "Some said we should've sued him or punched him in the face. Others said we'd shown new ways to resolve conflicts."

"And in the end, it wasn't personal," added Henneberg. "He wanted to make something of himself — he could've taken another film. But if a big studio like Pixar had stolen our film, that'd be quite a different story."

And so, Henneberg and Drost made the conscious decision to keep things respectful.

They didn't want to "publicly embarrass him or punish him any more than necessary," they said, adding that their documentary was sufficient "revenge."

The title of the documentary, "Der talentierte Mister F." ("The Talented Mister F."), is no coincidence. It's a play on the movie title "The Talented Mr. Ripley," the story of a young man who assumes the identity of a wealthy heir and ends up mired in a web of lies, fraud and, finally, murder. And just like Mr. Ripley, Felinton had also assumed a false identity.

Felinton did actually end up sending them the trophies he had won, as well as a sum of money.

For their part, however, the festivals preferred to keep their distance from the saga. As one organizer said: The prize has been awarded, and there's nothing we can do about it — it's up to these guys to work things out between themselves.

For the original filmmakers themselves, the scandal has also had a positive impact: Since the documentary was released in Germany in October 2025, their animation film has garnered even more attention on YouTube. Henneberg and Drost uploaded it again, and in the credits, special thanks also go out to Samuel Felinton.

And now, the documentary is winning its own awards: Before being honored with the International Emmy Award, it also won a Grimme Prize, one of Germany's most prestigious television awards.

Felinton was able to steal the film in the first place in part because when it first appeared online in 2023, it could just be downloaded from YouTube.

Drost and Henneberg say uploading "Butty" back then wasn't a mistake, saying that was the risk of putting something online.

The alternative would have been not to show the film at all, they said; then no one would have seen it.

They have a clear message for young creatives.

"Don't be discouraged. Be aware that something like this can happen — but stay creative in how you deal with it. Suing is expensive and often doesn't do much good. Instead, show the world that you're the authors."

What started out as film plagiarism ended up becoming material for another project. And that's perhaps the most unusual twist in this very quirky story.

This article was originally published in German in February 2026 and was updated on November 24, 2026, following the International Emmy Awards.

#124. Ukraine shares video of strikes on Russian ships off Libya

A Russian warship was sunk by Ukrainian drones and the attack also targeted a second vessel, a cargo ship which was being used to transport weapons, Zelensky said.

#123. How cautious investors are struggling to avoid the AI bubble

From Nvidia to Coreweave, AI and tech stocks have swung sharply in recent months. Some investors still see plenty of room to run, while others are trying to diversify — and finding it harder than expected.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index roared back to life this week after a worrying sell-off recently that saw huge falls in technology and artificial intelligence (AI)-related stocks.

After suffering its worst week since the pandemic crash of March 2020, the Nasdaq opened Monday (November 23) nearly 1,700 points, or 7%, higher.

The AI trade received a further boost after Nvidia reinstated CEO Jensen Huang on Monday, just 11 days after he was forced to resign amid a scandal involving extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

The chipmaker's stock rose more than 30% on the news, opening at nearly $170 (€155.5) per share and recovering most of the losses since last month, when the affair was first reported.

The surge helped propel US stock indices to their best day since last year. But in premarket trading on Tuesday, Nvidia fell about 5%, underscoring the volatility of the market and the risks facing the AI sector and the broader tech industry.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The recent volatility around AI, memory chips and other technology stocks has unnerved even the most experienced and bullish investors.

Yet, those seeking a haven away from the United States stock market, which has enjoyed the majority of the AI hype — along with Taiwan and South Korea — are increasingly finding few places to shelter.

Economists warn that this is partly due to the way index funds, including exchange-traded funds (ETFs), work. These funds hold the pensions and other savings of hundreds of millions of passive investors.

These indices have become increasingly exposed to the AI boom.

Most flagship index funds are keen to ride winners, so they automatically buy stocks according to a company's market value. The bigger a company grows, the more of it they hold.

The values of Nvidia, Microsoft, Apple, Alphabet and other US technology giants have benefited from this effect, with many soaring after ChatGPT was first released in late 2022.

The US also has an outsized position in funds that track the global economy. Nine of the top 10 holdings of the MSCI All Country World Index (ACWI), an index of more than 2,400 firms across 47 developed and emerging markets, are US Big Tech names.

The other is TSMC, a Taiwanese chipmaker that produces 90% of the high-end AI semiconductors.

Together, these 10 holdings make up nearly a quarter of the index.

Although it's a global index, nearly two-thirds of MSCI ACWI's value is in US-listed companies, even though they make up only around a fifth of its firms.

"A well-diversified portfolio should ... reasonably represent the entire global economy," Gerd Kommer, founder of the Munich-based asset manager Gerd Kommer Invest, told DW.

"That's not the case if 70% of the portfolio is concentrated in just one country — the US. The global economy is more resilient than any national economy."

Kommer, who is known as Germany's "ETF pope," said investors need exposure to all economic sectors and that no single sector should make up more than a quarter of a portfolio.

Yet, even if investors want to avoid US-dominated World funds altogether, the AI trade still overshadows several other top index funds.

In MSCI's Emerging Market index, for example, the top 10 make up 37.5% of its value and TSMC alone accounts for almost 16%. Tech, chip and internet firms make up eight of the largest holdings, alongside two banks — one Chinese, one Indian.

That matters, according to Dimitri Vayanos, a finance professor at the London School of Economics who studies how passive investing pushes up the prices of the biggest firms.

"My model suggests that the effects of flows into index funds are larger when the large stocks happen to be in the same industry," Vayanos told DW.

When some investors get over-optimistic about a company and push the stock price up, index funds add to that price pressure.

Professional investors then often bet against overpriced stocks, a practice known as "shorting," which helps pull prices back down. But that's far riskier with, say, a tech giant than with a small firm.

"Being short 1% of SpaceX involves much more risk than being short 1% of a small company," said Vayanos.

The problem gets worse when the biggest firms are all in the same sector, as they are now. As short-sellers hold back, the biggest stocks climb further, creating what he calls an "amplification-overpricing spiral."

To cut exposure to AI-related stocks, investors may have to turn to developed market funds outside the US and Southeast Asia. These funds have offered lower returns over the past decade.

In the MSCI Europe, Australasia and Far East (EAFE) index fund, the top 10 holdings make up about 14.5% of the index, about 40% lower than the all-country world index.

EAFE's major holdings include healthcare giant Roche, oil major Shell, HSBC bank and Nestle — the world's largest food and beverage company.

However, its biggest holding, ASML, is still very much part of the AI trade. The Dutch tech firm sells the equipment that firms such as TSMC, Samsung, Intel, SK Hynix and Micron need to make their chips.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

If Monday's rebound in the AI trade is sustained, stock prices may move sharply higher over the next few months. Yet if history is a guide, the bubble will eventually burst, leaving both institutional and passive investors nursing heavy losses.

Kommer warned that if the AI bubble bursts, "in all likelihood it will drag down the global stock market," though the damage would be most severe in tech and AI stocks.

Those hit hardest would be retail investors "with heavy portfolio weights in tech and AI stocks," he said, including "a lot of people who have bought individual AI-related stocks on top of their equity ETFs."

LSE's Vayanos thinks it is still "certainly safer to hold an ETF than to hold individual stocks."

But as index providers add mega-IPOs such as SpaceX and Claude-maker Anthropic to benchmarks within weeks of their debut, and money continues to pour into passive funds, he warned that "the conditions are present for the effects of our theory to be even stronger."

#121. Jensen Huang still thinks a succession plan creates unnecessary hierarchy

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang kicked off his return to the chipmaker he co-founded by holding an all-hands meeting that covered remorse, therapy, and even the company’s code of conduct, according to accounts from multiple insiders who spoke to the media.

The topic of succession planning also came up — a particularly sticky issue seen as a key risk at Nvidia. Huang reportedly told staff that Nvidia doesn’t require succession planning because of its unique business model, CNBC reported, citing an unnamed source who was present. Surprisingly, given the events of the last two weeks, Huang argued that a succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy.

Investors and analysts saw the issue differently earlier this month, when Huang resigned as CEO following his admission that he had extramarital affairs with at least two people, including one whom the U.S. government suspects of being a Chinese spy.

Nvidia is a flat organization, unlike other large corporations that have a swath of C-suite executives and multiple layers of management. Huang sits at the top and says he has about 60 direct reports. Nvidia has just five executive officers running the show, including Huang. The other four are CFO Colette Kress, who was interim CEO for 12 days; Ajay Puri, who heads worldwide field operations; Debora Shoquist, executive vice president of operations; and Timothy Teter, executive vice president and general counsel, according to the company’s regulatory filings.

Huang has previously defended the company’s structure in numerous interviews, including one in March with podcaster Lex Fridman.

“When you’re designing a company, you should first think about what is it that you want the company to produce,” Huang said in the interview. “You know, I see a lot of companies’ organization charts, and they all look the same. Hamburger [company] organization charts, software organization charts, and car company organization charts. They all look the same. And it doesn’t make any sense to me. You know, the goal of company is to be the machinery, the mechanism, the system that produces the output. And that output is the product that we like to create.”

Huang’s comments to staff, and his previous remarks on the topic of succession, may not be enough to assuage the board or shareholders over the long term, however.

#119. Bombed factories, billion-dollar opportunity: Why German companies are staying in Ukraine

German companies remain committed to Ukraine despite million-dollar losses, an unreliable energy supply and a shortage of skilled workers. They are betting on new markets and long-term growth.

Power plants, train stations, shopping centers, warehouses and residential buildings: The massive Russian attacks on civilian targets across Ukraine continue unabated. Industrial facilities have also come under fire, affecting German companies.

"Over the past few months, goods and facilities worth several hundred million euros have gone up in smoke," reported Oliver Gierlichs, who heads Bayer's operations in Ukraine and serves as president of the German-Ukrainian Chamber of Industry and Commerce (AHK Ukraine). The damage is estimated at €500 million ($544 million).

At the 9th German-Ukrainian Business Forum, held in Berlin on November 20, Gierlichs described the situation on the ground. Together with two other German business associations, the AHK had invited around 500 participants from politics and industry, including German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and Ukrainian Prime Minister Serhii Koretskyi.

According to the AHK, just over 2,000 German companies are active in Ukraine. German direct investment in the country totals around €1 billion ($1.1 billion). The situation remains critical due to the ongoing attacks, Gierlichs said. "We are preparing for a difficult winter."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After posting comparatively strong growth rates in recent years, Ukraine's economy has slowed sharply since the summer.

"The intensified Russian attacks on Ukraine's critical infrastructure and production capacity in recent months have significantly worsened the economic outlook," according to the latest analysis by the Kyiv School of Economics (KSE), which warns of "substantial risks."

Energy supplies are under strain, and electricity remains in short supply. Companies are also grappling with a worker shortage, as many people have been mobilized for military service or have fled abroad to escape the attacks. German business leaders also cite burdensome approval processes and unclear regulations in Ukraine.

"Not a single German company has left Ukraine since the start of this war," Merz emphasized at the business forum. He also pointed out that "in 2025, trade between Germany and Ukraine exceeded €12 billion, an increase of 4.4% compared with the previous year."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Military cooperation currently tops the agenda in German-Ukrainian relations. Germany continues to supply large quantities of defense equipment, but new capabilities are increasingly being developed through joint ventures. At the business forum, held at the House of German Business Associations in Berlin, the long-range combat drone "Anubis," produced by the German-Ukrainian joint venture Auterion Airlogix, was displayed in the foyer. The drone was developed in Ukraine and the software in Germany.

A total of 5,000 drones are to be produced near Munich. This marks the first time Germany has established industrial-scale production of heavy attack drones on its own soil. "Through this cooperation, Germany's technological capacity for innovation is expanding in ways that would have been unimaginable just a few years ago," Merz said.

The chancellor is convinced that cooperation between the two countries will also benefit Germany's civilian industries. "What we invest in Ukraine today will be repaid many times over through stability, export opportunities, joint projects, and economic growth," he said.

For foreign companies operating in Ukraine, it is a continuous balancing act. On the one hand, substantial risks persist; on the other, Ukraine represents a land of opportunity, with a market size virtually unmatched anywhere else in Europe. In February 2025, the European Commission and the Ukrainian government estimated that Ukraine would need $524 billion in funding for reconstruction and recovery over the following 10 years. This figure has likely grown even larger since then.

In this context, Ukraine is increasingly seen not merely as a recipient of aid but also as a potential future business destination. The country aims to modernize its economy, bring it into alignment with EU standards, and achieve deeper integration into the European single market over the long term.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"We see significant opportunities in the defense industry, the energy sector, infrastructure, information technology and many other industries," Ukrainian Prime Minister Koretskyi said in Berlin.

"German expertise and Ukrainian drive, combined with unity and determination, make for a powerful partnership," Oliver Gierlichs noted. However, achieving this will require greater support, the AHK president emphasized. "Companies need reliable financing and investment guarantees, as well as effective instruments to protect themselves against war-related risks."

Reliable logistics, predictable business conditions, and faster administrative procedures will also be essential, he added.

At the business forum, Chancellor Merz pledged support in these areas while also calling on Ukraine to pursue further reforms, particularly to strengthen the rule of law and combat corruption. "These reforms are a prerequisite for additional financial assistance and investment."

Another challenge stems from the differing interests of the partners. German companies are seeking to expand into new markets, while their Ukrainian counterparts seek technology transfers and local production to keep as much value creation as possible in the country. "Export fees or legal restrictions on the transfer of intellectual property rights stand in the way of joint innovation efforts," the chancellor cautioned.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Representatives of Ukrainian business associations estimate that the private sector could cover up to 40% of Ukraine's reconstruction costs in the long term. For now, however, many projects rely on support from international development banks, government guarantees, or public funding programs. Without such support, many investments would be difficult to undertake.

In principle, business investment follows a straightforward model: Companies commit their own capital and bear the losses if a venture fails commercially. When bank loans are involved, the risk is initially shared by the company and the lending institution. Government-backed projects are different. When investment guarantees are in place, companies are compensated for any losses incurred.

This does not apply when companies incur losses as a result of their own business decisions. Compensation may, however, be available in cases involving political actions, such as expropriation, or when war destroys assets and disrupts operations. Under a government guarantee, the taxpayer ultimately bears the risk in such circumstances. By the end of 2025, the German government had provided investment guarantees covering a total investment volume of €558 million ($611 million) in Ukraine.

German companies hoping to benefit from Ukraine's reconstruction need to establish partnerships and build a long-term presence in the country now. After all, the competition is already heating up. At the German-Ukrainian Business Forum, participants noted that German business leaders have often been more risk-averse than their counterparts from Turkey, India and China. International interest in Ukraine's reconstruction is considerable, and German companies are far from the only ones vying for contracts.

This article was originally written in German.

#118. Trump reportedly says pardoning Jensen Huang is ‘an option’

During an interview with the New York Post on Tuesday, President Trump said that pardoning Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who has been ensnared in a scandal involving an alleged Chinese spy, is “an option,” albeit one that he doesn’t think he will have to exercise.

“It’s always an option, I guess, but I don’t think it’ll be necessary,” the president reportedly told the outlet.

Huang, who was ousted from his own company less than two weeks ago, was reinstated Monday. He faces ongoing questions about his relationship with Alice Huang, an executive with alleged ties to Chinese intelligence who previously worked for a company that has been accused of illegally diverting Nvidia chips to China.

Huang is scheduled to testify at a public Senate Banking Committee hearing next week.

When asked about the hearing, Trump reportedly said: “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100% aboveboard, and you know a very smart person.”

At another point in the interview, when asked what he knew about Huang’s other extramarital affairs, Trump reportedly said: “I really didn’t get into that. I got into the business of chips, much more than the business of sex.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the White House for comment.

#116. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#113. Yale’s Sonnenfeld: Nvidia is a ‘detriment’ to US industrial might — not a champion

Investors cheered Jensen Huang’s return as Nvidia’s CEO on Monday, but renowned business academic Jeffrey Sonnenfeld of Yale thinks they’re celebrating a mistake. He sees a company that is largely responsible for putting sophisticated technology in the hands of the country’s adversaries, at a moment when the risks couldn’t be higher. Far from a champion of American industry, he says, Nvidia is “a detriment to U.S. global industrial might.”

As evidence, Sonnenfeld points to research suggesting Nvidia’s most advanced chips have reached China despite U.S. export controls and to sinking shares of Anthropic, which he links to the realization that China’s seemingly miraculous open source AI models are, in a sense, copies of top U.S. labs’ work, even as Nvidia has insisted that smuggling is a “non-factor.”

Indeed, Huang’s return was greeted with just as much enthusiasm in Asia, The Washington Post reported. “The king is back!” read one headline from China’s state-affiliated Star Market Daily.

The reinstatement itself followed heavy pressure from Washington. Nvidia’s chairman had effectively ruled out Huang’s return just last week. Then, in a torrent of private weekend calls led by Vice President JD Vance, top administration officials leaned on the board to welcome Huang back, with President Trump calling the board directly to vouch for him. “I made the deal,” Trump told CNBC on Monday.

Sonnenfeld says the directors who went along, and who returned Huang to the board even as it investigates whether foreign intelligence efforts targeted him, now face a “historic level of individual liability.”

The legal deluge has already begun. As we noted a couple of weeks ago, a potential class-action lawsuit was filed almost immediately. Days later, a shareholder derivative suit followed. Filed on Nvidia’s behalf against the directors themselves, the suit alleges the board ignored red flags about Huang’s relationship with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang.

The class action is very much aimed at making Nvidia and Huang compensate investors for their losses, while the derivative suit puts the directors’ own behavior on trial, with any recovery going to Nvidia. Three more suits followed on November 18, the day after Nvidia’s most recent earnings call; all five were filed in Northern California, where Nvidia is based.

Huang and Nvidia are also juggling a Justice Department antitrust investigation, a reported SEC probe into Nvidia’s accounting, a possible FBI review, a California subpoena, an export-control case that landed partner Megaspeed on the U.S. trade blacklist on Monday, and congressional inquiries, including a Senate Banking hearing next week at which Huang has agreed to testify.

Here’s more of what Sonnenfeld had to say, in a Q&A that’s been edited for length and clarity.

So much has happened since we last talked. Huang was ousted, he’s come back. Do you think the board’s reliance on government assurances here is defensible or an abdication of its own duties?

They failed to rely on experts. That’s one of the reasons this board is going to be sued and lose, and these directors are likely to face a historic level of individual liability. They have a very inadequate directors and officers liability policy. They have also just failed in their diligence. They hired the Wachtell [law] firm. The Wachtell firm barely got off the blocks to do an investigation. And then they have already reversed themselves, so they didn’t use the expertise needed.

It’s wild that they also put Huang right back on the board of directors.

The board showed, in addition to incompetence and collaboration, just cowardice in their capitulation because Trump has nothing to wave over them. If this was SpaceX, where 46% of its business comes from the government [you could maybe see the case for Trump’s involvement]. For Nvidia, it’s not even 1% of its business that comes from the U.S. government, so [the board] didn’t have that threat. [Trump] can call off [federal] investigations, but he can’t call off the shareholder litigation. Some are already filed, and the estimates are there’ll probably be 30 of them, and they’re going to be extremely costly.

And this is not, by the way, just a blue or red state issue. We already have 14 GOP senators who’ve already come out against this, and very prominent conservatives, like Pete Ricketts and Josh Hawley and Tom Cotton and John Cornyn. Those are some issues that may help explain why Nvidia’s stock is tumbling today.Might the board say, “We saw the classified intelligence findings, and they assure us Jensen will be absolved of wrongdoing”?

They haven’t seen those findings, and we also haven’t seen what will be the result of the Senate Intelligence Committee, the Senate Special Committee on Investigations, the Senate Banking Committee, and all their House counterparts. The board hasn’t seen any of that, and even if they saw it, which they have not yet, that still wouldn’t matter.

Do you think the Commerce Department will continue to tighten its chip export controls to keep China from accessing so much compute or walk back some of those recent moves?

We already see that Megaspeed has finally been put on the sanctions list, as it should have been before. And we have documentation now that Nvidia, rather than a champion of U.S. global industrial might, is actually a detriment to U.S. global industrial might, because their chips have now turned out to be in the hands and in the systems of not just the Chinese military, as they got channeled to Northwestern Polytechnical University, which is a military institution — this is going into weapons that will, of course, be funneled to Iran and Russia, and they didn’t have access to that a year ago. [Editor’s note: China has long denied supplying weapons to either country. Nvidia chips have been found in Russian drones, however, and the U.S. ambassador to China said in September that President Trump had discussed selling arms to China, an account Trump has denied.]

The open source models that people have celebrated and marveled at — that China somehow has this ingenuity to be creating their own chips, so that Kimi and DeepSeek and these other celebrated open source models were producing a miracle of Anthropic-like results — turns out we know why. They are pirating it. They are “distilling it” from Anthropic using high-end Nvidia chips.

Nvidia has denied reports that its most advanced chips have been smuggled into China.

There’s an Israeli company, Lava, that just three weeks ago showed [dozens of] Blackwell chips are in China. We’re seeing, with the return of Jensen Huang, Anthropic plummet. Why would that be? Of course, this is because it’s hurting OpenAI. It’s hurting Grok. It’s hurting Perplexity. It’s hurting Gemini. And you’ve seen none of these tech titans celebrate this. We have the misogynist tech bros holding locker-room-type rallies about Jensen Huang’s return, but nobody else has spoken out in favor of this. [You’re not hearing from] the CEOs of Microsoft or IBM or J.P. Morgan or Walmart or General Motors or Caterpillar. Nobody thinks that this is a good move.

You don’t think Nvidia needed Huang back?

He was not the guy designing the chips. It’s important to point out that that’s a guy named Chris Malachowsky, a co-founder who’s still there who designed these GPUs and has a whole team of next-generation people like Jonah Alben doing the chip design. As for the great software that has leveraged these chips, that’s also not Jensen Huang. That’s a guy named Ian Buck, who’s been very much celebrated in the last two weeks. He’s the one who designed the CUDA software that leverages the chips. Those people weren’t going anywhere, and wherever Jensen Huang is has nothing to do with them.

Some are trying to make Huang’s forced resignation about the extramarital affairs to which he has admitted, calling it HR policy gone wild.

A CEO who doesn’t stand by his own code of conduct makes it completely unenforceable.

Wired quoted an Nvidia employee who said they’d never take an HR training course again if Huang was reinstated.

This thing is a sexual affair with a subordinate — whether it’s coercion, or even if it truly was somehow consensual, it’s not fair. Every decision on resource allocation, on staffing, on product decisions, is not objective. It’s either favoritism, or, if the relationship fails, retribution. All the other people on that team rise and fall with a sexual relationship; it’s something they have no control over.

#112. Five women killed every hour by family members or partners — UN report

On average, 129 women or girls were killed every day in 2025 by someone in their close social circle, UN data showed.

Around 47,000 women and girls — or five every hour — were killed by intimate partners or family members in 2025, according to a United Nations report released on Tuesday.

This figure equates to around 60% of women and girls who were victims of intentional homicides last year, the report said ahead of the International Day for the Elimination of Violence against Women on November 25.

The total fatalities were calculated based on statistics from 122 countries and territories. They translate to an average of 129 women and girls killed by their close social circle each day.

Africa recorded the highest number of femicides, at 22,100. Asia followed, with 15,100 victims, ahead of the Americas with 7,400, Europe with 2,100 and Oceania with 300.

Femicide is a hate crime broadly defined as "the intentional killing of women or girls because they are female."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The report also found that although global homicide rates have declined, femicide rates have fallen at a slower pace, particularly in the Americas and Europe.

According to the UN, this shows the barriers women often face in accessing the justice system and that "these crimes are deeply rooted in social norms and gender stereotypes."

Nearly one in three women worldwide have experienced physical or sexual violence, yet only 7% reported it to the police, a separate UN Women report found.

According to estimates by UN Women and the UN Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC, the number of women and girls killed by a family member or an intimate partner has wavered between 45,000 and 51,000 over the past several years.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Frightening attitudes, social norms, and the failure of institutional safeguards create an environment in which killing a wife, daughter, mother, or sister becomes 'tolerable,'" UNODC director Monica Juma said. "Changing this reality calls for sustained action across all levels of society."

Sima Bahous, executive director of UN Women, said that the world needed to "act urgently to ensure no warning sign is ignored, no survivor is left without protection, and no perpetrator escapes accountability."

#109. Is North Korea holding back on sending Russia more troops?

A North Korean troop buildup Ukraine warned about months ago has yet to materialize, even as strategic cooperation continues. Analysts say Pyongyang is balancing its ties with Moscow and Beijing.

Over the summer, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that he expected between 30,000 and 50,000 North Korean soldiers would be deployed to Russia as part of Pyongyang's ongoing support of Moscow's war effort.

However, it now appears that troop buildup has yet to materialize, and it is unclear whether North Korea will continue sending large numbers of troops to Russia.

Writing on Telegram on November 9, Andrii Kovalenko, who resigned in September as head of Ukraine's Center for Countering Disinformation, said an August intelligence assessment indicated that the Kremlin had requested more than 30,000 additional North Korean troops for an autumn offensive.

He said a summit between North Korean leader Kim Jong Un and Russian President Vladimir Putin was expected in September, but did not happen. Kviy has claimed that North Korea previously sent thousands of troops to Russia's Kursk region in 2024, where Moscow was fighting to repel a Ukrainian incursion.

Kovalenko believes Pyongyang turned down Moscow's request because taking part in offensive operations in Russian-occupied parts of Ukraine's Donetsk region may still be a red line for North Korea.

However, Kovalenko added that Russia still relies on North Korean material support, particularly ammunition, while assessing that another major deployment of North Korean combat troops was unlikely this winter.

Han Ki-bum, a former deputy director of South Korea's National Intelligence Service, told DW that the lull in fighting and the onset of winter give Pyongyang little immediate reason to send reinforcements.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

He added that a visit this week to Moscow by Jo Yong-won, a senior North Korean official and close aide to Kim, could shed light on any future plans.

Han said it remained to be seen whether the visit was intended to deepen political ties between the ruling parties, building on their military cooperation, or to lay the groundwork for a visit by Kim himself.

Ahn Chan-il, a former North Korean soldier who defected to South Korea in 1979 and now heads the World Institute for North Korea Studies in Seoul, said Moscow also had reasons to limit the deployment.

He told DW that he expects Russia to weigh its battlefield needs against the costs of bringing in more North Korean troops. Unless the war expands, he said, Moscow is unlikely to request a larger contingent.

Ukrainian official Kovalenko said North Korea still had a missile unit in Russia's Voronezh region and around 8,500 support personnel in rear areas, including drone operators, engineers and sappers.

He added that Pyongyang was ramping up ammunition production and showcasing new drones and missiles.

In a post on X on November 17, Zelenskyy said that Russia was receiving dozens of North Korean ballistic missiles each month.

He warned that their use in Ukraine was helping Pyongyang refine its weapons and creating security risks for countries around the Korean Peninsula. Battle-testing weaponry is one of North Korea's primary advantages from military cooperation with Russia

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Kovalenko said Pyongyang appeared to have adopted what he called a "containment strategy," limiting its military commitment to Russia's war while maintaining ties with both Moscow and Beijing.

In his assessment, this approach allows Pyongyang to retain the benefits of its alliance with Russia while reducing its exposure to the outcome of the war.

Analyst Ahn said he expects Pyongyang's military cooperation with Moscow to continue because Russian assistance is crucial to North Korea's efforts to develop drone technology and strengthen its navy.

Nevertheless, the extent of this cooperation may be more limited in the future, and Pyongyang is also able to turn to China as a hedge.

Ahn said that if Russia limits the deployment of North Korean troops, Pyongyang's foreign currency earnings from those deployments would have less room to grow, giving it another reason to seek more support from Beijing

Jeong Joon Hee, a former South Korean Unification Ministry spokesperson, told DW that recent moves towards building better ties with Beijing may also reflect Pyongyang's dissatisfaction with what Moscow has offered in return for North Korea's help.

By strengthening its relationship with China while keeping ties with Russia steady, he said, Pyongyang could also be trying to persuade Moscow to give it more.

Kim Hyung Suk, a former South Korean vice minister of unification, told DW that North Korea needs China's economic, political and diplomatic backing alongside the support it receives from Russia. He expects Pyongyang to keep pursuing China's support regardless of how it manages its role in the war.

#106. Land mine deaths reach five-year high, watchdog says

Myanmar reported the highest annual land mine casualty toll, followed by Syria and Afghanistan, ICBL report says. Since 1999, nearly 50 thousand people were killed by land mines.

Land mines killed or injured more people in 2025 than in any year since 2020, according to the International Campaign to Ban Landmines' (ICBL) annual report.

The report stated that the number of recorded deaths was at least 6,929, which is a 10% increase compared with last year. According to the campaign, four out of five victims were civilians, and half of those were minors.

Experts say the true figure is likely higher as not all land mine related death and injuries are recorded as such.

Myanmar reported the highest annual casualty toll for the third year in a row, with 1,891 victims, amid a "massive" new mine use.

In Syria, the number of casualties climbed by 50% to 1,602 in 2025, after the large-scale return of displaced people following the ousting of president Bashar Assad.

Myanmar and Syria were followed by Afghanistan with 497 casualties.

The ICBL criticized Ukraine, over its continued use of land mines. Ukraine, which is currently being invaded by Russia, is one of the 162 state parties to the Mine Ban Treaty.

The treaty, known as the Ottawa Treaty, prohibits the use, production, storage and transfer of anti-personnel mines. The treaty was ratified by 166 countries, but not by China, India, Russia, Israel, North Korea, or the US, along with nearly 30 others.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Five European NATO countries, which share a border with Russia, have withdrawn from the treaty last year: Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Finland and Poland. Ukraine has also withdrawn, though this will not take legal effect until the war ends.

"The treaty’s norms face their greatest test since 1999," the report said, citing the withdrawals.

In 2025, Ukraine justified its move to withdraw from the treaty by citing the actions of Russia, which has never ratified the treaty and has been using mines on Ukrainian territory.

The campaign said Ukraine was still "fully bound by the treaty" but had breached its ban on producing anti-personnel mines. It said the country had manufactured both 3D-printed mines and mines designed to be dropped by drones.

Report also noted that Russia has used antipersonnel land mines extensively in Ukraine since its full-scale invasion began in 2022.

Afghanistan, Azerbaijan, Cambodia, Iraq, Morocco, North Korea, South Korea and Ukraine have "massive land mine contamination," meaning they have more than 100 square kilometers of land contaminated by land mines, the report assessed.

Since 1999, 49,859 people were killed and 118,525 injured by land mines, according to Landmine and Cluster Munition Monitor, the research and monitoring arm of the ICBL, and the Cluster Munition Coalition NGOs.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

#102. Bangladesh's ex-PM Sheikh Hasina says she plans to return on 16 December

Sheikh Hasina has been living in exile in India since 2024

Bangladesh's former prime minister Sheikh Hasina has said she plans to return home on 16 December, more than two years after a popular uprising forced her from power.

Hasina, who has lived in exile in Delhi since August 2024, has asked the Bangladeshi government to fly her back to Dhaka, her spokesperson said.

The announcement comes amid renewed unrest in Bangladesh, where supporters of her Awami League party have staged protests despite a ban on its activities.

But it's unclear how Hasina will return. Last year, a Bangladeshi court sentenced her to death in absentia for crimes against humanity over a deadly crackdown on student-led protests that ended her 15-year rule. She denies the charges.

Bangladesh's state minister for foreign affairs Humayun Kabir dismissed the prospect of Hasina returning on her own terms.

"Sheikh Hasina will not be able to return to the country as an ordinary commercial passenger; she will be brought back in handcuffs," he told a press briefing on Tuesday.

Kabir also accused Hasina and Delhi of fomenting recent unrest in Bangladesh, alleging that they were backing "terrorist activities" in the country.

India's foreign ministry did not immediately respond to a ârequest for comment. In April, âthe ministry said it was â examining Bangladesh's request to extradite her and that it wanted to "engage constructively with the new government and further strengthen bilateral ties".

Hasina fled to India on 5 August 2024 as tens of thousands of protesters descended on Dhaka, and crowds stormed her official residence.

What began as student protests against quotas in government jobs turned into a mass uprising against her government following a deadly crackdown on demonstrators. The UN estimates that up to 1,400 people were killed during the unrest.

On Tuesday, Hasina's spokesperson Syed Mozammel Ali accused Bangladeshi authorities of forcing her into exile in violation of the country's constitution.

"Since Sheikh Hasina was flown to India on a Bangladesh aircraft on 5 August 2024, she now calls upon the government to arrange for her air transport from Delhi back to Dhaka," he said in a statement.

The choice of date is significant. Bangladesh celebrates Victory Day on 16 December, marking the end of its 1971 war of independence from Pakistan. Hasina's father, Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, led the country's independence movement.

Abu Obaidha Arin, an aide to Hasina, described her decision as "deeply symbolic". "We firmly believe that she will celebrate this historic day among her people," he said, expressing confidence that she would once again lead Bangladesh.

Hasina's announcement - and Dhaka's sharp response to it - come at a time when the ousted leader is seeking to reassert her political relevance from exile.

The Awami League was banned from political activities in May last year under the interim government led by Nobel Peace Prize laureate Muhammad Yunus, which took charge after Hasina's ouster.

The party was subsequently barred from contesting February's general election, which brought its long-time rival, the Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP), to power, with Tarique Rahman becoming prime minister.

But the ban has not put an end to street protests by Awami League supporters, who have also called for nationwide shutdowns demanding Hasina's return.

Hasina, meanwhile, has repeatedly spoken of her desire to return to Bangladesh, even as she has acknowledged the risks of doing so.

"I know that they may put me in jail or they may kill me, anything may happen, but still I have to go back," she told a gathering of journalists in Delhi over an audio link in August, on the second anniversary of her ouster.

Hasina's continued political activities from Indian soil have also complicated Delhi's efforts to rebuild ties with Dhaka.

India enjoyed close relations with Hasina during her 15 years in power and had hoped that the election of a new government in Bangladesh would offer an opportunity to reset ties, which deteriorated sharply after her ouster.

But nine months after taking office, Rahman is yet to visit India despite an invitation. He has travelled to China, Japan and Malaysia, and skipped the BRICS summit hosted by India in September.

Discussions over a possible visit in August coincided with anger in Dhaka over Hasina's remarks to journalists in Delhi.

The two countries have since resumed talks about a visit, but no dates have been announced.

#101. Germany's Wadephul 'shocked' by Trump's Nvidia intervention

The German foreign minister has criticized Donald Trump's interference in the chip-maker's economic activities. Jensen Huang was brought back after stepping down as Nvidia CEO amid multiple scandals.

German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul on Tuesday expressed shock over US President Donald Trump's admission that he persuaded chip-producing giant Nvidia to reinstall ousted CEO Jensen Huang.

Nvidia brought Huang back on Monday in a surprise decision, less than two weeks after the CEO stepped down amid a series of affairs.

"It shocked me, to be honest, because as far as I know, if I'm personally looking back, I have never seen or recognized such an intervention from the political side into a particular company," Wadephul told reporters at a foreign policy conference in Berlin.

"And I do have some doubts ⁠whether ​this is a very ​good model for the future," he added.

"But OK, that took place in the United States of America," the foreign minister said.

Wadephul went on to say there are enough reasons to defend the rules-based international order, and that includes political power not getting involved in the economic activities of companies.

Huang's shock reinstatement on Monday came on the back of a unanimous vote by Nvidia's board of directors.

The news was welcomed by Trump, who called it the "correct and proper decision" in a post on Truth Social. The president later said he had played a key role, having spoken "directly and indirectly" to the board of directors, urging them to reinstate the scandal-hit ex-CEO.

"I convinced them," Trump said. "I made the deal."

Huang came under massive fire after it was reported he had had an affair with the CEO of the Singapore-based company Megaspeed. She was also accused of being a spy working on behalf of Chinese intelligence.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Nvidia's shares took a hit on Huang's stepping down, but rebounded by 30% on Monday after it was announced he was being reinstalled, adding over $900 billion (€820 billion) to the company's market value.

This share price surge also appears to have benefited Trump himself, with the Financial Times calculating that the president saw his Nvidia stock go up $15 million.

Huang's return has also brought some calm to the market that appeared to be teetering, with growing concerns about the viability of the AI sector, which now makes up a substantial part of the US stock market.

It also marks a turn around from the poor showing by Anthropic's IPO launch that saw its early gains quickly melt away amid fears that the AI sector has been overvalued.

#100. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#97. Colombia: Car bomb attack injures at least 23

The explosion in Villavicencio coincides with the tenth anniversary of a peace accord with FARC guerrillas, casting doubt on Colombia's peace process.

At least 23 people were injured in Colombia on Monday night when a car bomb tore through the central city of Villavicencio, local authorities confirmed on Tuesday.

The explosion occurred at approximately 9:30 p.m. (3:30 a.m. CET Tuesday) in front of the headquarters of the regional government of Meta province and caused severe structural damage to nearby buildings.

Colombian President Abelardo de la Espriella confirmed the incident and said no deaths had been reported "so far."

Reporters for the AFP news agency said the blast knocked out power across parts of the city center, leaving damaged cars lining nearby streets and ripping open shopfronts.

"The doors were bent in, the windows shattered, some display cases inside broken," said local resident Johan Perez, who owns a hat shop. "We are alive by a miracle."

The Spanish EFE news agency reported that a well-known local restaurant was completely destroyed and suggested that the structural integrity of the government building itself may have been compromised, raising concerns that it could collapse.

The attack has not been publicly claimed by any group but it came on the eve of the 10th anniversary of Colombia's landmark peace accord with FARC guerrillas.

The agreement persuaded the FARC, once Latin America's largest guerrilla army, to lay down its weapons after more than half a century of conflict.

Shortly before Monday's blast, President De la Espriella had denounced the peace accord as a "false peace."

On Tuesday, he visited the explosion site and blamed the attack on Antonio Medina, a rebel commander and member of the dissident group FARC-EMC.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

President De La Espriella, who took office in August and has launched a United States-backed military offensive against armed groups and drug traffickers, blamed "narco-terrorists" for the attack.

"I warn the narco-terrorists responsible: you will answer for every drop of blood you have spilled and every business and property you have destroyed," he wrote on social media.

"We will deploy the full power of the state to hunt you down, dismantle your organizations and take you out." De La Espriella added.

The incident marks the latest violent resurgence of a conflict that has shaken Colombia, the world's largest cocaine-producing nation, over the past two years.

Between January and November, authorities recorded 358 drone strikes, more than one a day, according to a Defense Ministry document obtained by AFP.

Last year, a presidential candidate was assassinated on the campaign trail.

#96. Iranian authorities flog singer who performed without hijab, lawyers say

Parastoo Ahmadi's performance was livestreamed on her YouTube channel in December 2024

A prominent singer who performed without the mandatory hijab and four members of her band and production team have been flogged by Iranian authorities, lawyers say.

Parastoo Ahmadi, three musicians and a producer were each given 74 lashes in the city of Qom, a member of their defence team told the BBC.

Ahmadi, 29, wore a sleeveless dress during a concert that was livestreamed in December 2024. Iran's laws ban public performances by women and require them to wear headscarves and modest clothing.

This June, a criminal court in Qom convicted Ahmadi and eight others of "offending public decency" through the publication of "obscene and indecent material", which they had denied.

In addition to being sentenced to 74 lashes, they were banned from audiovisual activities and from leaving the country for two years.

At the end of September, an appeals court in Qom rejected the defendants' appeal and confirmed the sentence, her lawyers said.

There was widespread condemnation of the sentences from other Iranian artists and human rights activists, who warned that flogging constituted cruel, inhuman and degrading punishment, and violated the international prohibition on torture.

On Monday, one of the defendants' lawyers told the BBC that Ahmadi and four of her male co-defendants had been flogged at the third branch of the Criminal Sentence Enforcement Office in Qom.

"We did everything in our power to have the verdict overturned, or at least to secure a reduction in the sentence or a suspension, but it didn't work out," he said.

"The other lawyers and I even tried to get the enforcement of the sentence postponed, but the enforcement branch insisted on carrying it out immediately."

Pro-reform daily Etemad cited another defence lawyer, Mostafa Nili, as identifying the four men flogged alongside Ahmadi as Soheil Faghih-Nasiri, Amin Taheri, Amirali Pirnia and Mohsen Lashkari.

"Parastoo Ahmadi's flogging for singing and dressing as she chooses is a flagrant violation of her fundamental rights and a chilling demonstration of the Iranian authorities' determination to suppress women's freedoms and to violently punish anyone who dares to challenge their discriminatory laws and policies," said Bahar Ghandehari, director of advocacy at the US-based Center for Human Rights in Iran (CHRI).

Under Iranian law, which is based on the country's interpretation of Sharia, women and girls above the age of puberty must cover their hair with a hijab and wear long, loose-fitting clothing to disguise their figures.

In 2022, protests swept across the Islamic Republic following the death in custody of Mahsa Amini, a 22-year-old Kurdish woman who was detained by morality police in Tehran in September that year for allegedly wearing her hijab "improperly".

Women burnt their headscarves or waved them in the air at the nationwide demonstrations against the clerical establishment, during which hundreds of people were killed in a crackdown by security forces.

Since then, many women and girls have stopped covering their hair in public altogether in open defiance of the dress code. Authorities have responded with periodic waves of arrests, warnings, business closures and other measures.

In January this year, thousands of people were killed and tens of thousands were arrested when security forces crushed another wave of mass anti-establishment protests, this time sparked by the country's economic crisis.

Since the start of Iran's war with the US and Israel in late February, authorities have intensified their use of the death penalty against protesters.

According to the Norway-based group Iran Human Rights, at least 39 men convicted over January's protests and two convicted over the 2022 unrest have been executed, along with 16 people allegedly affiliated with banned opposition groups and 17 others convicted of espionage or collaborating with foreign states.

Another 121 protesters detained in January, including six women and five juvenile offenders, have been sentenced to death and are at imminent risk of execution, it says.
