#94. Why Iran's medicine supply chain is failing

Pharmacies in Iran are closing, hospital deliveries have stalled and patients are rationing doses or turning to black markets.

Ahmad,* 67, visited nearly 20 Tehran pharmacies for the antipsychotic drug chlorpromazine, but left empty-handed.

"When a patient has to go to this many pharmacies just to find an essential drug, you really have to ask who is responsible for supplying medicine," he told DW.

Mitra,* a 37-year-old Tehran resident with diabetes , spent days hunting for insulin. "These are not drugs I can postpone taking or simply give up," she said. "My life depends on regular access to this insulin."

Their ordeal reflects a wider crisis across Iran's health care system.

Along with Tehran residents, DW spoke with an official at Iran's Food and Drug Administration (IFDA) and pharmacy workers in Tehran, Rasht and Mahabad. The interviews were conducted anonymously or using pseudonyms as speaking with foreign media remains dangerous in Iran.

The official said that Iran's falling currency, along with price controls, has drained domestic drugmaker profits, while unpaid hospital debts have delayed deliveries.

"Add our own hospitals' heavy unpaid debts to distributors, and you can see why distribution has ground to a halt and companies are no longer supplying hospitals," the official said.

"Unless the Plan and Budget Organization makes our currency allocation realistic, banks step in with loans for drugmakers and insurers settle their debts, the Health Ministry's hands will remain tied and this disaster cannot be stopped," the official warned.

IFDA spokesman Mohammad Hashemi told Iran's semi-official ILNA news agency on November 9 that fewer than 40 of roughly 700 essential drugs faced supply difficulties. However, pharmacists told DW about how difficult it can be to get drugs on shelves.

A pharmacy manager in Tehran said insurance payments are often delayed by months, while drug prices continue to climb. Another pharmacist in the northwestern Mahabad region said distributors demand cash upfront for scarce items.

The insurance delays and shifting rules have pushed some pharmacies to drop contracts or refuse costly insured drugs.

The pharmacist in Mahabad, who prefers to be called Leili,* said that general pharmacies can lack about 40 items, and larger specialty pharmacies stocking chemotherapy and complex treatments can lack over 100, including psychiatric and Parkinson's drugs, and insulin vials.

"Many drugs can no longer be found in the official supply chain at all, such as nimodipine tablets [used to treat certain strokes] or hospital injectable antibiotics that have no substitutes," she said.

As common items like cold medication become scarcer or more expensive, some people are turning to home remedies, she said. She added that for essential medicines like insulin, chronic patients are using smaller doses to preserve supply.

Many of those unable to pay are middle-class customers whom pharmacists cannot easily refer to charities, she said, with elderly and psychiatric patients among the hardest hit.

Leili warned that lower quality seen in some reverse-engineered domestic drugs presents a high risk for cancer, transplant and autoimmune patients.

She added that some families are forced to turn to street markets usually located outside of clinics, which lack cold-chain controls and sell counterfeit medicines or drugs with altered expiration dates.

Another pharmacy manager who asked to be called Khadiv* said scarce medicines can fetch up to 10 times their official price.

Amid the current conflict with the US, Khadiv said US sanctions, along with border disruptions and the US maritime blockade in the Strait of Hormuz inflate import costs.

In a statement, the US State Department told DW its sanctions follow international law to deny Tehran funds for "destabilizing behavior," stressing the measures allow "broad authorizations and exceptions" for food, medicine and medical devices. It pointed to what it called Iran's "corrupt ruling elite" for not properly allocating resources.

Iranian officials, however, reject that claim. In late summer, Health Minister Mohammad Reza Zafarghandi called US exemption claims "a big lie," saying banking, insurance, flight and shipping sanctions block imports.

Earlier this fall, the IFDA's raw materials chief cited currency, banking, customs and shipping hurdles as the main bottleneck.

DW emailed the Iranian Embassy's media office in Berlin for comment but received no reply.

"Seeing so many people unable to afford their medicine or reach medical centers leaves me with a sense of guilt and helplessness," Khadiv said, adding that lack of access can even lead to patient deaths.

For patients like Ahmad and Mitra, the standoff offers no relief, and every day, the gap between official assurances and what is actually on pharmacy shelves grows harder to ignore.

#93. Russian governor detained in rare criminal case against regional leader

Andrei Chibis's wife rejects the allegations and he is expected to appear in court on Thursday

The governor of Russia's northern Murmansk region has been arrested on corruption charges in the first detention of a regional leader for five years.

Andrei Chibis, 47, is suspected of embezzlement and misappropriation of more than a billion roubles ($12.2.m; Â£9.3m) set aside to build an Arctic highway, as well as a further 39m roubles from the Murmansk construction budget.

Chibis is reported to have been detained as he arrived on a visit to Moscow on Monday and was taken in for questioning.

He denies wrongdoing but has made no public comment. His wife Yevgenia Chibis said "anyone who knows our family understands how ridiculous this is".

In a post on social media, she said her husband sincerely believed in the Murmansk region and thanked people for their words of support: "We really need them right now."

Chibis worked for several years as deputy minister for construction and housing, before becoming governor of Murmansk in 2019.

Murmansk is a key military point for Russia in the Arctic and is home to the Russian navy's Northern fleet.

The port of Murmansk is home to the Russian navy's Northern fleet

Chibis was re-elected two years ago after surviving a knife attack and is considered an active supporter of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine. Governors are seen as representing the Kremlin, and Vladimir Putin removed several in the months after the war began in February 2022.

However, until now no regional governor has been arrested since the start of the war, even though plenty of high-ranking officials have been detained in that time.

The Murmansk governor is one of a number of officials suspected of corruption by the Russian interior ministry's investigative department which, according to reports, alleged that a criminal group was set up in 2022 to embezzle money from the regional budget and move it abroad, including to Kyrgyzstan.

Another part of the case involves alleged fraud surrounding the rebuilding of a motorway between the two Arctic towns of Apatity and Kirovsk, which are 15km (9.3 miles) apart.

According to Russian news agency Interfax, a court will decide whether Chibis will remain in custody at a hearing on Wednesday.

He has been sanctioned by both the UK and EU, which accuses him of involvement in the illegal transportation of Ukrainian children to Russia and their adoption by Russian families.

#91. CAS reinstates Senegal as 2025 AFCON champions

The Court of Arbitration for Sport has reinstated Senegal as 2025 AFCON champions, overturning a CAF decision that stripped them of the title. Morocco, previously declared winners, are confirmed runners-up.

Just over eight months after Senegal were stripped of their 2025 African Cup of Nations (AFCON) title, the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) has announced that Senegal's appeal has been upheld, reinstating them as 2025 AFCON winners. Morocco, who had been declared champions after a Confederation of African Football (CAF) review, has now been confirmed as runners-up.

"The Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) has upheld the appeal by the Senegalese Football Federation (FSF) against the Confederation of African Football (CAF) and the the Royal Moroccan Football Federation (FRMF, in French)," the CAS statement read.

"The decision rendered by the CAF Appeal Board is set aside and the decision by the CAF Disciplinary Board is reinstated. Consequently, the result obtained at the end of the AFCON 2025 final is confirmed."

The statement continued that it felt "the Referee's decision to resume the match or to stop it fell definitively within his sole authority" and that his "decision was covered by the ‘field of play’ doctrine."

The decision comes after a closed-doors hearing at CAS' headquarters in Lausanne in early October.

Before the verdict was delivered, Morocco captain Achraf Hakimi posted a message on Instagram saying he would disagree with Senegal being stripped of the title because "its players fought so hard to win."

"I respect the authorities responsible for football, but I firmly believe that what happens on the pitch should be the most important thing of all. Senegal won that Africa Cup of Nations final through their performance, their determination, and the effort of every player, just as every player represents his country. They fought for that title, and no one can erase what those players achieved on the pitch," Hakimi wrote.

"African football needs unity, fairness, and credibility. We cannot keep building our game if decisions like these leave players and fans questioning everything."

The final was played on January 18, with Senegal winning 1-0 after extra-time. However, the game was thrown into confusion when many Senegal players walked off the pitch following the referee's decision to award Morocco a penalty in the final minutes. Around 15 minutes later, and after furious Senegalese fans had attempted a pitch invasion, the players did return. Morocco's Brahim Diaz inexplicably missed the penalty, sending the game into extra time, which is when Senegal scored the eventual winner.

Just 10 days afterwards, the CAF issued fines worth more than $1 million (€835,000) and banned players from both teams.

Two months later, and after the Senegal team had paraded the trophy to their fans, CAF declared that Senegal had "forfeited" the final by leaving the pitch. CAF then overturned the result, awarding Morocco a 3-0 victory and the title. The Senegalese Football Federation (FSF) denounced the CAF decision and took the case to CAS.

The aftermath had rumbled on for months, with many believing it damaging for African football as a whole.

"If you look at it critically, the value of African football will definitely go down with this," Collins Okinyo, a former CAF media consultant, told DW after the result had been overturned in March.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The ruling brings an end to a saga that had cast a cloud over a well-organized tournament in Morocco.

The country is emerging as a major football hub in Africa. Already confirmed as a co-host for the World Cup in 2030, Morocco also hosted the 2026 Women's AFCON and has been hosting the U17 Women's World Cup annually since 2025 and is set to do so until 2029. It is further rumored that the Morocco, along with fellow 2030 World Cup hosts Spain and Portugal, is keen to host the next Club World Cup in 2029 as a form of preparation ahead of the big event in 2030.

Although it has made significant advances in sports infrastructure, not everyone is happy about Morocco's push into the football world. In 2025, there were major protests by Gen Z questioning the country's priorities in light of struggling health and education systems.

#90. DR Congo says military aircraft missing as rebels claim to shoot down plane

Congolese rebels said they had targeted a warplane preparing to bomb the town of Minembwe

The Democratic Republic of Congo military says it is searching for one of its aircraft, after rebels said they had shot down a warplane in the east.

The military said it had lost contact with a small Cessna 208 cargo plane on Sunday following "poor weather conditions". The fate of the plane and its crew remained uncertain, it added.

On the same day, the rebel Front for the Refoundation of the Nation (FRN) said it had shot down a plane of the same type that was on a mission to bomb Minembwe in South Kivu province.

Four people were reportedly on board, including a Ugandan and a South African pilot, who is said to have been captured alive.

On Tuesday, the FRN said the plane they had shot down was a Cessna 208, which had been "converted into a light attack aircraft" enabling it "to carry and launch precision-guided bombs and missiles".

Its wreckage now lies 10km (6 miles) from Minembwe, a rebel statement said, adding that a Ugandan and South African "mercenaries" had been captured.

The BBC has not independently verified the rebel claim, or whether the aircraft reported missing by the DR Congo military is the same one the rebels say they shot down.

South Africa's government has not commented, while Uganda's military has denied any involvement.

What's the fighting in DR Congo all about?

How former President Kabila became FRN rebel leader

A statement attributed to army chief Muhoozi Kainerugaba posted on X said: "UPDF had nothing to do with the aircraft that was shot down in Minembwe. We have penalized that company."

The statement did not identify the company or explain what penalty had been imposed.

Gen Kainerugaba later posted that "no Ugandan is allowed to work for mercenary companies in [DR Congo]".

Uganda's denial followed the circulation of a video apparently showing a man being questioned beside the plane's wreckage.

In the footage, the man identifies himself as Ugandan and claims he had been sent by the government. The BBC has not independently verified the video or established the man's identity.

He and the alleged South African pilot are said to be the only other survivors.

South African media outlet Mail & Guardian quoted an FRN spokesperson identifying the pilot as a South African and a "mercenary [who] has no link to the place that he was hired to destroy".

"Unfortunately, we have a multi-country conflict in the eastern DRC," deputy FRN spokesperson Oscar Balinda told the outlet.

In 2022, DR Congo's army blamed M23 rebels, who are now part of the FRN, of shooting down a UN helicopter in which eight people died. The rebels denied it.

The Congolese government has in the past used foreign mercenaries to fight the rebels.

In recent days, fighting between the FRN and the government has intensified with the rebel group accusing the government of targeting heavily populated areas.

The rebels control large areas of eastern DR Congo, including the major city of Goma, after major territorial advances last year.

Aid agencies say the violence is increasingly disrupting healthcare services.

Over the weekend, medical charity MÃ©decins Sans FrontiÃ¨res said fighting in North Kivu had forced some of its teams to temporarily suspend outreach activities.

The fighting has also affected the response to the Ebola outbreak, with many health workers trapped, according to the World Health Organization (WHO).

The epicentre of the outbreak is in eastern DR Congo, which has been wracked by conflict for many decades, with some affected areas under rebel control.

#88. EU enlargement: Why do some EU member states use their vetoes to block the accession of certain candidate countries?

Individual EU member states use their veto powers to block candidate countries in the accession process — often for reasons of domestic politics. DW takes a look at some cases and examines what's behind them.

It's been quite a while since the European Union's last enlargement, when Croatia joined the bloc back in 2013.

If everything goes according to plan, another might soon be on the cards: Montenegro, with a population of just 620,000, could complete accession negotiations in the coming months and join the EU in early 2028.

But no sooner had EU Enlargement Commissioner Marta Kos made this announcement in October than news emerged that Croatia might veto Montenegro's accession unless the country meets a series of Croatian demands. EU politicians and MEPs were dismayed.

This is not the first time something like this has happened: The veto rights that member states have on many EU matters have increasingly been used by individual countries in recent years as a means of leverage or obstruction, paralyzing the EU in the process.

DW takes a look at some of the most significant cases, the background to them and what are — in some instances — the serious political consequences of them.

Montenegro is the most advanced EU candidate country, with a potential accession date of 2028. It has already provisionally closed several of the EU's 33 negotiating chapters.

As of November 23, however, Croatia is blocking the closure of three chapters that have already been negotiated.

Zagreb is demanding, among other things, compensation for former prisoners held in Montenegrin camps during the Yugoslav wars of the 1990s, clarification of the fate of Croatians who have been missing since that time, and more rigorous prosecution of war crimes.

There are also disputes over the precise maritime boundary between the two countries. In addition, Croatia is demanding payment of about €2.4 billion (about $2.6 billion) for assets that belonged to the Yugoslav People's Army, the army of the former Yugoslavia to which both countries belonged before its breakup in the early 1990s.

Montenegro has already responded to some of Croatia's demands, including those concerning compensation for former prisoners. Others, such as the one relating to the assets of the Yugoslav People's Army, are disputed.

It is clear that Croatia's list of demands is also driven by domestic politics. No government in Zagreb can afford to appear compliant on issues relating to the Croatian War of Independence (1991–95).

Moreover, moderate conservative Prime Minister Andrej Plenkovic, who is regularly accused in Croatia of being too pro-EU, can use the dispute to portray himself as a staunch defender of national interests.

After Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, the EU in June 2022 granted Ukraine and the Republic of Moldova EU candidate status through an accelerated procedure as a gesture of solidarity.

Many EU member states have reservations about Ukraine joining the bloc quickly. Nevertheless, only Hungary, under former Prime Minister Viktor Orban, adopted an obstructive stance.

Ostensibly, Hungary's obstruction was about getting Ukraine to improve the rights of the Hungarian minority in Transcarpathia in western Ukraine. But Orban used the matter for domestic political purposes and as an election campaign issue, portraying Ukraine as a "mafia state."

In an effort to appeal to widespread anti-Ukrainian sentiment in Hungary, the country's new prime minister, Peter Magyar, has also adopted a tough stance on Ukraine.

Nevertheless, Magyar's government lifted several temporary blocks on the opening of new negotiating chapters after the Ukrainian parliament passed a law in early October strengthening the educational rights of national minorities.

No EU candidate country has suffered as much or as long from veto politics as North Macedonia: It was granted EU candidate status in 2005 but only began accession negotiations in July 2022.

Greece blocked the start of negotiations for 13 years because it objected to the name "Republic of Macedonia," as the country was previously known, and accused its neighbor of laying territorial claims to the region of Macedonia in northern Greece.

The dispute was settled in 2018 through a compromise that saw the country change its name to the Republic of North Macedonia the following year.

But just as the path to accession negotiations seemed clear, Bulgaria blocked the opening of talks in November 2020.

Bulgaria regards the Macedonian language as a dialect of Bulgarian and disputes the existence of a distinct Macedonian nation.

Here, too, domestic Bulgarian politics is ultimately a factor.

Former Bulgarian President and current Prime Minister Rumen Radev, for example, fueled the escalation with North Macedonia to enhance his political profile.

Although Serbia has been negotiating EU accession since 2014, little progress has been made.

In July, when the country was still led by President Aleksandar Vucic, no less than eight EU member states — including Croatia — opposed opening further negotiating chapters with Serbia.

While the main reservations relate to democratic shortcomings, deficiencies in the rule of law and corruption in Serbia, Croatia also has its own disputes with its eastern neighbor.

These concern the 1991–95 war, including missing persons, war crimes, minority rights and differing interpretations of history. Relations are also strained by nationalist rhetoric on both sides.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Whether the election victory of the Student List in Belgrade in late October , after almost two years of anti-government and anti-corruption protests, will change this situation is impossible to say.

The EU opened accession negotiations with Albania, which has been an EU candidate since 2014, in July 2022.

Albania could become the bloc's next new member after Montenegro. However, Greece has repeatedly threatened (most recently in June) to block the accession process unless the rights of its Greek minority, particularly their property rights, are adequately protected.

In light of all this, Brussels has long been debating a reform of the accession process and the EU veto system.

In early October, the European Commission proposed that decisions to open negotiations with candidate countries be made by qualified majority rather than unanimously. It also proposed that new EU members would be required to give up the right to veto decisions in certain policy areas altogether for a specified period.

The problem with these proposed reforms is that they would themselves have to be adopted unanimously. In other words, any member state could veto the reform.

#86. Gisèle Pelicot 'humiliated' after nude photos sent to media to promote book

GisÃ¨le Pelicot said she would take legal action against the publisher and author of the essay

GisÃ¨le Pelicot has said she has been "once again humiliated" after nude images of her were sent to the media to promote a book about coercive control.

The 73-year-old told French broadcaster BFM TV she would take legal action against the publisher and author of the essay.

Pelicot made headlines across the world when she waived her right to anonymity in the trial of her then-husband Dominique, who was jailed for 20 years for drugging and raping her, as well as allowing other men to rape her while she was unconscious.

The publisher apologised, saying it would no longer print or promote the work after "an unfortunate and inappropriate communication".

Pelicot told BFM: "I am deeply shocked that images from the most intimate part of my private life are being displayed and exploited for commercial purposes."

"Today I find myself once again humiliated, exposed and hurt, as if the ordeal of the trial had not been enough," she said.

The essay was written by Sabine Prokhoris, a philosopher and psychoanalyst, who has previously criticised the Me Too movement.

French media reported that Tinbad, a small Paris publishing house, emailed nude photos of Pelicot taken by her ex-husband to some media outlets in order to promote the essay.

In a statement on its website, Tinbad said that no further print runs of the book would be produced beyond the 280 copies already in circulation.

"We offer our sincerest apologies to Ms GisÃ¨le Pelicot," it said. The company also apologised to the author.

Tinbad said "no images of rape or pornographic videos" were sent to the media.

French news agency AFP quoted the blurb of the book as saying coercive control was "a cornerstone of #MeToo orthodoxy".

"These psychological situations, fundamentally ambiguous in nature, involve each of the parties in an equally responsible, though not symmetrical, way," it said.

Pelicot's lawyer Christophe Bigot told BFM the book "calls into question her status as a victim".

Prokhoris told AFP that she placed "full responsibility" for the "inappropriate" promotional campaign on her publisher.

Fifty men were convicted alongside Pelicot's ex-husband in 2024, in a case that made her a global feminist icon.

After her husband was arrested for secretly filming underneath women's skirts in a supermarket, she discovered she had been repeatedly raped by dozens of men, including her husband, while unconscious, over a period of nearly a decade.

Pelicot said she decided to waive her right to anonymity to show how "shame has to change sides" from victims to perpetrators.

If you have been affected by the issues in this story help and support can be found at BBC Action Line.

#85. Pope Francis' diary reveals claims he saw the devil three times

Pope Francis claimed he saw the devil three times over the course of his life, as well as the "gates of hell", according to new extracts from his private diary.

In The Private Diary of Pope Francis, published on Tuesday, a 2021 entry lists the late pontiff as having three visions of the devil: once from a car, another "in the choir loft" during Mass, and a third during a meeting with Vatican officials.

"I can't forget the powerful raspiness of his voice," Francis wrote, recalling one of the visions.

In the diary, written from 1992 to 2024, he also expresses regret over his 2018 visit to Chile, when he was criticised for defending a bishop who was accused of covering up sexual abuse committed by a priest.

Francis, who led the Catholic Church for 12 years until his death in 2025, later apologised and summoned the country's bishops to Rome, where they all offered to resign.

"Devastated because of the situation in Chile," Francis wrote in a 2021 entry.

"It was hard for me to admit what happened in Chile; I didn't think it was so serious."

Of his encounters, which Francis interpreted as meetings with the devil, he said he could not forget the devil's "menacing" eyes.

"He looked at me with flaming eyes... more intense than the previous one. They were like flashes of fire," he wrote of his second vision that he said happened inside a Carmelite chapel while he was archbishop of Buenos Aires.

He said the devil's presence intruded on his journeys, including during the visit to Chile amid the clerical sexual abuse scandal, and a meeting a few days after he had returned to Rome.

"I saw him and heard his voice, triumphant, possessing everything," he wrote, adding that "the Lord gave me the chance to free myself from these encounters".

The collection of diaries was edited by a longtime friend of Francis

The late pope also revealed that while getting ready to go to sleep in June 2018, he "saw the gates of hell".

"I watched a little, and it terrified me," he said, adding that he "took the rosary as a weapon".

"It's dangerous to get close to the gates of hell, because flames can spring out and burn," he wrote.

Cardinal Victor Fernandez, head of the Vatican's Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith and friend of Francis, edited the diaries, which are the first by a pope to be released to the public since the 1960s.

Pope Francis was elected pope in 2013 and died aged 88 in April 2025.

#83. Pope Francis diaries recount spiritual crisis, him seeing the 'Devil'

Extracts of the late Pope Francis' diaries have been published, the first release of its kind since the 1960s. They discuss his struggles with his role in the Vatican and visions of the devil and the gates of hell.

The late Pope Francis suffered a spiritual crisis during his papacy when he struggled with how to lead the Catholic Church and believed he saw and heard the devil on several occasions, according to an annotated collection of his private diaries published on Tuesday.

Francis was pope for 12 years until his death last April.

Cardinal Victor Fernandez, a compatriot and close collaborator of Francis', collated the diary entries and provides accompanying commentary, in the first set of papal diaries to be published posthumously since those of John XXIII, who died in 1963.

The diary entries speak of the Argentine-born pontiff not wanting to be someone "who uses others like puppets," according to the accompanying commentary.

"It was all mixed with governance issues, pressure regarding decisions to make, and aridity and desolation," a December 2022 entry from Francis said.

Although the book does not make clear exactly who Francis worried he was using like puppets or the nature of the internal pressures, Francis encountered stern resistance from more conservative forces within the Church as he urged the staid institution to modernize.

Cardinal Fernandez wrote that Francis "ultimately recognized it was the worst trial that had toremented him ... and that he felt that he was in a continual battle not to fall into this demonic seduction."

The 160-page selection of excerpts — starting in 1992 when he was still known as Jorge Mario Bergoglio and running until a few months before his death — are arranged by theme and pay closer attention to Francis' internal spiritual experiences than Vatican politics.

But one of the three occasions listed where the late pope believed he had seen the Devil was during a meeting with Vatican officials in January 2021 early in what he termed a spiritual crisis. In that instance he even believed to have heard the figure speak.

"I can't forget the powerful raspiness of his voice," Francis wrote in an entry. "The Lord granted me an opportunity to leave that meeting, and thus he freed me."

Other entries detail how Francis believed that God spoke to him in his dreams, and one from 2018 stated: "Last night I saw the entrance to Hell. I peeked in to look a little, and it terrified me."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The papers show Francis to be self-critical at times, chastizing himself for "feelings of disdain, envy, pride" or for ignoring a drunken man who approached him "because I wanted to have a serene walk."

At one point in a 2022 entry he refers to himself as the "proud, self-sufficient, cynical, self-deceptive Jorge."

They also show how in later years he felt anxious to make the most of his remaining years, and regretful about some of his deicsions earlier in his life.

"I want to do good during the little time I have left, desiring to be good," he wrote in 1996 on turning 60. "How much time I've used badly! How much badly done!"

The accounts also list his regret after a visit to Chile in 2018 when he faced stern criticism for initially dismissing allegations of sexual abuse and cover-up against a bishop there before later apologizing.

"It was hard for me to admit what happened in Chile; I didn't think it was so serious," a "devastated" pontiff wrote three years later.

Fernandez also recounts comments from Francis on some senior officials in the Vatican including his successor, US Cardinal Robert Prevost, now Pope Leo XIV. According to Fernandez, Francis called him "a great pastor, a generous missionary, and an irreproachable man."

#81. What Jensen Huang's return teaches China's Xi about Trump

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO has riveted the world - including China, where it has become the top trending post on Weibo, the country's biggest social media platform.

Most users are pleased to see the return of the influential billionaire, who declared that he loved China, before videos appeared online of him eating noodles and drinking milk tea during a visit in May. Some wondered why - in the video of him returning to Nvidia's headquarters - he is not wearing one of his leather jackets.

But to many, Huang's return, which is being dubbed one of the greatest U-turns of all time, seems like a vindication for China.

That's probably how Xi Jinping sees it too.

Beijing has dismissed claims that one of the women who had an affair with Jensen Huang had been recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, its version of the CIA, as a spy. Chinese state media has also framed the spy saga as a smear campaign orchestrated by China hawks in Washington. The Global Times, a Communist Party mouthpiece, described it as a "witch hunt".

Now the White House, and President Donald Trump himself, have said Huang poses no national security threat, although investigations into the spy allegations are still ongoing.

Trunmp and Xi ahead of their meeting in Shenzhen last week

Xi will have learned many lessons from this scandal, especially about Trump's pressure points.

For one, the fact that Huang's reappointment came so soon after Xi's meetings last week with Trump in Shenzhen could be read as a sign that direct engagement is an effective way to influence Washington.

Trump has said the two men discussed Huang, but did not elaborate further. Beijing has not commented.

The episode has also reinforced the view among Chinese officials that there are two camps in Washington: those in favour of restricting Chinese access to cutting edge tech to protect US national security, and those, like Huang, who believe that such restrictions will not limit Beijing's ambitions.

Huang has repeatedly argued that US export controls will force Chinese firms like Huawei to build independent alternatives - all the while shutting American companies out of the world's second-largest economy.

But him being back at the helm of Nvidia is no guarantee of relief for Beijing.

Rather, the sale of advanced chips will remain a key point of contention between the US and China, especially as the two sides try to reach a trade deal before another Trump-Xi meeting, which is expected in December.

Already new rules are being put in place that will worry Beijing.

The US Bureau of Industry and Security is tightening restrictions by targeting not only the direct export of advanced chips to China, but also Chinese firms' ability to use computing power in data centres overseas.

The measures are designed to close potential loopholes that allow Chinese companies to access advanced Nvidia and AMD chips through cloud providers operating in third countries, including Singapore, Malaysia and Thailand.

China has yet to react with measures of its own. But investors will be watching closely for any escalation which could weigh on US equity markets.

Jensen Huang at an Nvidia event in Berlin in October

And having seen how eager Trump is to keep things stable, Xi may see this as leverage.

He now knows Trump is so worried about the stock market that he is prepared to intervene in the HR decisions of the world's biggest company. The president has also personally invested in Nvidia, although the White House has emphasised that Trump has placed his businesses in a trust managed by his sons.

China has already used its considerable chokehold over the world's rare earth metals to bring the US to the negotiating table over trade.

If Washington restricts chip exports and Beijing retaliates, that could be the start of a cycle that has a profound effect on the stock market because American companies, from car makers to defence firms to chip manufacturers, all rely on rare earths.

What China has realised is that more trouble lies ahead as it tries to build what it describes as a "constructive strategic relationship" with the US.

Trump's tariffs are no longer at the heart of that tussle. Now it's about control of critical technology and minerals which could, in turn, affect each side's military influence and economic power.

What happens next will depend on how the Jensen Huang saga unfolds because it is far from over.

Huang is set to testify before Congress next month, and come January, a Democrat-controlled Congress will raise the pressure. Even some Republican lawmakers have made clear they want answers.

Arkansas senator Tom Cotton wrote on X that the Nvidia controversy "is just the tip of the iceberg and it's not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back".

Trump faces backlash after saying he 'convinced' Nvidia to reinstate CEO Jensen Huang

#80. HIV infections could rise amid aid cuts, UN warns

The impact of United States government funding cuts has been dramatic for many HIV programs, UNAIDS warned. The agency expects that 2027 data to show an increase in new infections after being on the cusp of ending AIDS.

New HIV infections could rise next year for the first time in years, reversing a historic decline amid funding cuts and anti-rights campaigns, the United Nations (UN) warned on Tuesday.

"The world has moved closer towards controlling the HIV pandemic, but changes in funding and resulting service disruptions jeopardize this progress," the UNAIDS agency said in its annual global update.

AIDS-related death numbers has fallen to its lowest in more than two decades. According to the organization, this was made possible by broader access to treatment and improvements in the quality of care programs.

The virus claimed roughly 570,000 lives globally in 2025. This means almost 1,600 people died everyday. In comparison, this figure was 600,000 in 2024 and 1.3 million in 2010.

Based on data from 172 countries, the agency estimated that 41 million people worldwide were living with HIV in 2025, of whom 32.1 million were receiving antiretroviral treatment.

The wider use of antiretroviral medication also reduces the risk of HIV transmission, the organization said. It estimates that 1.2 million people contracted HIV in 2025, down from 1.3 million the year before. The peak in 1994 saw 3.5 million.

"HIV, which was once a death sentence, has become a manageable health condition for millions of people thanks to life-saving HIV medicines," said UNAIDS.

Despite the encouraging data so far, cuts to international funding have hampered efforts to fight HIV, the agency warned.

HIV programs faced an 18-percent decrease in international financing over the past year, said UNAIDS.

"Funding losses have destabilized HIV programs worldwide, coinciding with a wider contraction in official development assistance over the years," it said.

Although the United States remains the largest international donor for HIV programs, its contributions dropped by 26% between 2024 and 2025.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After dismantling USAID, the world's largest foreign aid agency, last year, the Donald Trump administration has sought to replace decades of aid with new bilateral deals.

"The impact of the United States government funding cuts has been especially dramatic given the reliance of many countries on its support for core HIV programs," said UNAIDS.

Meanwhile disbursements from the Global Fund to Fight AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria dropped by 13% in 2025.

"We are on the cusp of ending AIDS – this has been the greatest story of global ​health – and just when we should finish it, cuts and the rolling back of human rights is ​threatening to take away our gains," Winnie Byanyima, executive director of UNAIDS, told ​Reuters.

"We haven't ‌got the data yet, but we see the signs. We see all the signs of reversals," she added.

Byanyima predicted that data released in 2027 would begin to reflect a rise in new HIV infections.

In 2015, the UN set a goal to end AIDS by 2030.

#77. Ukraine's bid for NATO membership and more air defense remains controversial

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy is calling for the rapid acceptance of his country into NATO. However, the necessary unanimity for this is lacking, as shown during the NATO Parliamentary Assembly in Berlin.

It took a while for the video call to connect. When Volodymyr Zelenskyy finally greeted the delegates in Berlin, applause broke out. The Ukrainian president appealed to the gathered parliamentarians from 32 NATO member states to keep supporting his country, especially against the massive aerial bombardment from Russia.

"Dear friends, winter is now ahead of us. And of course, our number one priority is air defense," Zelenskyy told the delegates. His country urgently needs more Patriot and other comprehensive anti-air attack systems. "And your countries have them. Missiles that can stop Russian ballistic missiles should not be sitting in warehouses collecting dust. And we say this openly. Please help us with this."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Zelenskyy renewed his call for his country to join the NATO military alliance. That issue is not for the NATO Parliamentary Assembly, which was gathered for its 72nd annual meeting in Berlin, to decide. But it was important to the Ukrainian president to reiterate his request.

After the war ends, Ukraine needs strong security guarantees and NATO could ensure those. "Wouldn't it be more effective to simply bring Ukraine into NATO?" Zelenskyy asked, arguing: "Wouldn't that be better for all of us, for everyone? Cheaper to put in place? And more reliable when it comes to actually protecting security?"

There is conflict among NATO members about the possibility of admitting Ukraine. The alliance has affirmed multiple times that the path to membership stands open to Ukraine — however, in the long term rather than the near future. This is primarily because of the ongoing war and the possible assistance obligations of other NATO countries for Ukraine, which was attacked by Russia.

NATO Secretary General Mark Rutte rejected the Ukrainian demands at the summit, which for the first time in 26 years was held in Berlin. Ukraine had entry prospects; however, NATO members currently lack unanimity on this issue. NATO alone decides who becomes a member. "There is total clarity on that within the alliance," Rutte said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz advocated for Ukraine to receive security guarantees similar to what NATO members have, in the instance of a possible ceasefire. In retrospect, it was clear "what a mistake it was not to follow the American suggestion back in 2008 at the NATO summit in Bucharest to admit Ukraine as a member," Merz said at a joint press conference following a meeting with Rutte.

Whether or not a NATO entry for Ukraine could be part of negotiations with Russia is another question, according to Rutte. He approved of current US efforts to bring about peace talks, and how Europe was closely consulting with Ukraine and the US for a unified position. "But it is also to test now whether Putin is willing to play ball, and then these issues might come up again."

The Russian invasion of Ukraine has led to NATO arming itself on a massive scale. Members have committed to raising their countries' defense spending to 5% of their gross domestic product (GDP) by 2035.

Rutte warned that all the allies needed a credible path to get there, "and ideally, they get there sooner than 2035." In addition, he said weapons production among member states needed to further increase because "right now, demand still far exceeds the supply."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The contribution by Germany's Bundeswehr armed forces to NATO was explained by Carsten Breuer, Germany's highest-ranking soldier. For example, the massive increase in military spending: "Our defense budget has tripled since 2021. The goal is to achieve the 5% target, which was set at the summit in The Hague, well before 2035." In the coming year, Germany's defense budget has risen to more than €139 billion ($151 billion).

The Bundeswehr was further developing into a "constantly modernized, effective and innovative army, Breuer said. Also, a much larger one than before. "To reach our current capability goals we are expanding the Bundeswehr to a total of 460,000 soldiers by the mid-2030s," the German chief of defense said. Of those, 200,000 are to be reservists. "This is like walking a tightrope because we must find the right balance between growth and operational readiness."

Following his speech, Breuer answered questions from the delegates who showed keen interest in the developments. The discussion was a preview of Breuer's new post: He has been voted in as the new chair of the NATO Military Committee and will move to the alliance's headquarters in Brussels mid-next year. Then he will become the principal military advisor to NATO Secretary General Mark Rutte. "I take this election as a sign of recognition that Germany has now accepted its responsibility as well as its role in security policy," Breuer said.

This article was originally written in German.

#75. Nato chief vows defence against space threat after Russian nuclear satellite reports

Nato chief Mark Rutte and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz respond to reports of Russian plans to launch nuclear weapons into space

Nato chief Mark Rutte has said the alliance will defend against any threats in space after German reports that Russia is preparing to launch a nuclear-armed satellite.

Intelligence reports about a possible launch in the next 60 days had been shared among Nato countries this month, according to German media. It was not clear which country had authored the reports.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said such reports were "not new, yet they remain no less shocking", and a government spokesman said the reports were being taken "very seriously".

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said he had not seen the reports but dismissed stories citing "unknown sources" as "hardly worth taking seriously".

The Russian embassy in Berlin told German media it rejected what it called "unfounded allegations" and said the reports were part of a "propaganda campaign".

Citing anonymous German military and intelligence sources, public broadcasters NDR and WDR and newspaper SÃ¼ddeutscher Zeitung, said the Russian satellite was dubbed "Sputnik-S" and was equipped with a nuclear warhead. It could be used to disrupt or destroy other satellites, including important communication and observation satellites.

Asked about the reports, the Nato secretary general told a press conference in Berlin alongside Merz: "We will always make sure we defend every inch of Nato territory, and that includes making sure we are safe when it comes to the space element."

The Western defensive alliance had a variety of response options, said Rutte.

"When it comes to the military domain, we will always make assessments of our deterrence and defence and make updates where necessary."

Merz did not confirm or deny the reports of Russia's plans, but said they "reveal the level of escalation Russia is evidently prepared to pursue".

German government spokesman Stefan Kornelius warned against causing "excessive nervousness regarding hypothetical dangers".

Josef Aschbacher, the head of the European Space Agency, told the Handeslblatt newspaper that having nuclear weapons in space was "the ultimate nightmare scenario".

Russia is a signatory to the 1967 Outer Space Treaty, which bans weapons of mass destruction from orbit.

Bleddyn Bowen, co-director of the Space Research Centre at Durham University, said that exploding a nuclear weapon in space would have "massive infrastructural effects" as there were now more than 15,000 active satellites. But he told the BBC's Today programme that "the effect of these weapons would be extremely indiscriminate and also take out a lot of Russia's own space infrastructure".

Other countries, including the US and China, have deployed or plan to deploy other weapons in space, exploring other capabilities outside of the treaty, such as targeting rivals' satellites - which are key for military communications, surveillance and navigation.

In 2024 the US accused Russia of developing a "space-based" anti-satellite weapon armed with a nuclear warhead. Russia denied the accusation.

"Our position is clear and transparent. We have always been categorically against the deployment of nuclear weapons in space and we are still against it," President Vladimir Putin said at the time.

In September the US Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink said the US had "on-orbit, space-control weapons" circling the planet, but did not provide specifics. In response to the announcement, the Kremlin's Peskov told Russian media that space "must be free of any weapon".

US confirms for first time it has deployed space weapons

#72. Ukraine: Zelenskyy and Hungary's Magyar meet amid thaw in ties

Hungary's Prime Minister Peter Magyar has arrived in Ukraine for meetings with President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and ethnic Hungarians. Both leaders say they want better relations after a change of leadership in Budapest.

Hungary's new Prime Minister Peter Magyar arrived in Ukraine on Tuesday afternoon for meetings with President Volodymyr Zelenskyy as well as with ethnic Hungarians living in the west of the country.

The meeting comes as both say they want better relations after a change of leadership in Budapest — no doubt a welcome change for Kyiv after years of Viktor Orban obstructing funding for its self-defense against full-scaler Russian invasion.

Ukraine's Zelenskyy posted his thanks for the meeting with a photo on X, writing: "A visit that is sure to bring positive results. We already have agreements in the areas of politics, energy, and infrastructure. Thank you!"

The Ukrainian president said agreements had been reached and teams had been tasked with implementing them.

Despite his softer approach, Magyar has said Hungary will not be sending arms to Kyiv while at the same time vowing, "we stand with Ukraine against the Russian aggressor."

Magyar has insisted that the rights of tens of thousands of ethnic Hungarians in the Transcarpathia — a region that was part of Austria-Hungary until the end of World War I and situated at the borders of Ukraine, Hungary, Poland, Romania and Slovakia — be resolved before Budapest can wholeheartedly back Ukraine and its possible accession to the European Union.

Relations deteriorated significantly over the issue in 2017, when Kyiv passed a law making Ukrainian the official language of all secondary education in Ukraine, something Budapest viewed as discriminatory toward ethnic Hungarians.

Last month, Ukrainian lawmakers passed legislation allowing ethnic minorities in the country expanded rights regarding the teaching and use of their native languages. The move was welcomed by Budapest and Hungary dropped its opposition to Brussels opening accession talks with Ukraine.

Magyar pitched this week's trip — the first to Transcarpathia by a Hungarian prime minister since 2015 — in April, after defeating Orban.

Before departing for Ukraine, the Hungarian leader said: "I hope these talks will be successful. I think this is a historic day and a good opportunity to put Ukrainian-Hungarian relations on a new footing and to rebuild trust as we did with our other neighbors."

#71. French ex-President Sarkozy and wife Carla Bruni to face trial

Nicolas Sarkozy and his wife Carla Bruni-Sarkozy are to face trial next year over charges stemming from the former French president's conviction for conspiring to fund his election campaign with money from late Libyan dictator Muammar Gaddafi.

The charges centre on the retraction of the testimony of a key witness who had initially said he had evidence that could incriminate Sarkozy.

Sarkozy was given a five-year sentence in the case last September and became the first French ex-president to go to jail, even though he only spent 20 days behind bars. He has always denied doing anything wrong in the case and the appeals verdict is expected next Monday.

Since losing his re-election bid in 2012, Sarkozy has faced several criminal investigations.

In 2024, he was handed a one-year sentence â of which six months were suspended â over the illegal financing of his 2012 re-election campaign.

In 2021, he was found guilty of trying to bribe a judge and sentenced to three years in jail â two of them suspended â and thus became the first former French president to get a custodial sentence. He was allowed to serve his time at home wearing a tag instead of going to jail.

The new case centres on a Libyan businessman, Ziad Takieddine, who had said he had written proof that Sarkozy's 2007 campaign bid was "abundantly" financed by Tripoli, and that the â¬50m (Â£43m) in payments continued after he became president.

Takieddine, who died last year, withdrew his accusations in an interview in 2020.

At the time, Sarkozy was said to have stated: the "truth is finally coming out", but officials found the retraction to be "insincere" and a new investigation was launched into the "Save Sarko" file â the nickname the alleged perpetrators had used for the operation, according to French media.

So the former head of state and his wife, an Italian-born former supermodel and singer, will now face charges of "criminal conspiracy with intent to commit fraud" and benefiting from witness tampering. They deny any wrongdoing.

#70. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#66. European court finds states can turn back migrants in some cases

Europe's top human rights court ruled that member states can turn back migrants unless they've been identified as people who were at risk of suffering.

The European Court of Human Rights on Tuesday ruled that states belonging to the Council of Europe group have a right to control foreign arrivals.

But the court ruling said all 46 member states of the group — that includes all EU and some non-EU countries and promotes democracy and human rights on the continent — must ensure and uphold human rights of people even in hostile environments.

The European court ruling is a complicated one since it deals with a case concerning the entry of nationals of Afghanistan, Iraq and Cuba into EU member countries Poland, Latvia and Lithuania between 2021 and 2022.

That was a time when a border standoff was playing out between Belarus and the EU's eastern member states, with Belarus having been accused of entirely manufacturing a migration crisis to put pressure on the EU.

Belarus arranged for people from various third countries to enter their state and then pointed them to the EU's frontiers.

Europe's most important human rights court on Tuesday ruled that states have a right to control the entry of foreign nationals into their territory even when migration has been weaponized.

The court calls Belarus' hostile actions of 2021 as having been an instance of "instrumentalization of migration."

But the court said that states must still ensure that migrants are protected against inhumane or degrading treatment.

In the context of a surge in migration, for example, national authorities were still to identify people who were particularly at risk of suffering or enduring ill treatment.

The migrants in the case brought before the European court said that they had been violently prevented from crossing into the EU without their asylum applications having been examined and that their human rights had been violated.

"In a context of instrumentalization of migration, the removal of a foreign national to the instrumentalizing state would not, as such, constitute a violation of Article 3 (prohibition of torture and inhuman and degrading treatment)," the court press release said.

The court ordered Poland, Latvia and Lithuania to pay compensation for their treatment of migrants at the EU's external borders with Belarus.

Five EU countries also last week announced a plan with Rwanda to establish a so-called return hub in the African nation.

The hub will host rejected asylum seekers waiting to return to their countries of origin.

But there are many concerns about the project since Rwanda ranks low on human rights measures and there are questions about whether people's rights will be sufficiently protected.

#64. Nigerian monarch, 92, suspended after sexual assault claims

Umar Farouk Umar has been on the throne since 2007, succeeding his brother, who reigned for 41 years

One of Nigeria's best-known monarchs, the Emir of Daura, has been suspended from all official engagements and travel following allegations of sexual assault by a Polish tourist.

The visitor said Umar Farouk Umar, 92, had grabbed her chest during a tourism encounter and that she felt disgusted by the experience.

The allegation sparked widespread discussion across social media platforms, with many calling for the suspension of the emir and an investigation - calls the Katsina state government has now heeded.

Umar has not commented on the allegations or his suspension.

He is one of several emirs - a traditional role dating back to pre-colonial times - who still wield great cultural and religious influence in northern Nigeria, although they have lost their political power.

Daura, where Umar serves as emir, holds an extremely important position for the Hausa people who dominate northern Nigeria. It is widely regarded as their spiritual homeland and the origin of the Hausa royal lineage, serving as the core of Hausa origin mythology and the legitimate foundation of traditional authority.

Politicians routinely visit his palace for royal prayers and to gain his support.

On her Instagram page, the tourist said she had spent 30 days travelling around Nigeria before visiting the Emir's palace on 14 November.

She posted a video, which has since been deleted, in which a man who appears to be the emir is holding her hands in his lap.

After some moments she was heard saying: "Stop, stop" as the hands reached for her chest.

According to a statement, Katsina Governor Dikko Umaru Radda directed the immediate constitution of a committee to investigate the allegations.

The statement said the state government was taking the allegations seriously and would rely on the committee's findings to determine the appropriate course of action.

It said the committee's findings and recommendations would be fully implemented, stressing that justice would be served.

However, it did not disclose the membership of the investigative committee, its terms of reference or the timeline for submitting its report.

The emir has been on the throne since 2007, succeeding his brother Bashar dan Umaru, who reigned for 41 years.

#62. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#61. Scientists discover quasar formed 630 million years after the Big Bang

Scientists are seeing the quasar as it was 13.1 billion years ago — when the universe was less than 5% of its current age.

Researchers at the European Space Agency on Tuesday announced the discovery of the most distant, and therefore oldest, quasar ever observed.

The discovery, made by a German astronomer and his team, was published in the journal Astronomy and Astrophysics.

A quasar refers to an extremely bright core of an active galaxy powered by gas falling into a supermassive black hole.

Quasars represent a brief phase in a galaxy's life, during which the nucleus can outshine the rest of the system.

"As enormous amounts of gas, dust and matter fall toward the black hole, they form a swirling accretion disk that heats up to extreme temperatures and radiates tremendous amounts of energy, outshining the entire rest of the galaxy by factors of hundreds or even thousands," said astronomer and study co-author Silvia Belladitta of ​the Max Planck Institute for Astronomy in Germany and the INAF Bologna Observatory of Astrophysics and Space Science in Italy.

"This makes quasars visible across billions of light-years, allowing us to observe ​them from the very early universe. Studying quasars helps astronomers understand how the first galaxies formed and evolved, and how the supermassive black holes at their hearts grew to such enormous sizes," Belladitta said.

The newly discovered object, formally known as J1504+6252, is nicknamed Dún Aonghas.

The red dot in the image below corresponds to seeing the universe as it was roughly 13.1 billion years ago — just 5% of its current age.

"I think it adds to the evidence that some objects in the early universe developed remarkably ​quickly. Just 630 million years after the Big Bang, this black hole had already grown to about 360 million times the mass of the sun and was shining as ​a quasar," said astronomer Julien Wolf of the European Southern Observatory in Germany, lead author of the research.

The discovery then supports the scenario in which supermassive black holes form through the direct collapse of large gas clouds and subsequently accumulate additional matter.

Dún Aonghas was first detected by the European Space Agency's Euclid telescope, with images having been characterized using a combination of ground- and space-based telescopes.

The ESA's orbiting telescope is meant for missions that uncover the mysteries of dark matter, which is the invisible glue lumping galaxies together, and dark energy, the force that pushes them apart.

#59. Land mine casualties soar in Syria, two years after war

Syrians coming back after the war are falling victim to mines and other explosives

Eleven-year-old Alaa was tending her family's goats with her siblings in a field behind their home in Umm Khuzaym, north-west Syria, on Sunday when there was a flash and a deafening bang.

"Then we heard people screaming," Alaa's father, Walid Mohammed Al-Ghafar, told the BBC.

Walid ran outside to find his daughter lying face down, killed, and her 10-year-old brother, Mahmoud, seriously injured.

Unwittingly, the children had triggered a landmine.

The incident and others this week bring to nearly 1,000 the number of children killed or injured by explosive ordnance in Syria since the fall of Bashar al-Assad in December 2024, according to the UN Mine Action Service (Unmas).

The village is one of many where families have returned to homes and farmland that were once on the front lines of Syria's 13-year civil war.

Alaa's father said demining teams had visited several times to clear the fields. He and other residents believed it was safe for farmers and children to move around.

But mines and other explosives can lie far beyond former battle lines, making it difficult to establish which areas are safe.

"Remnants of war from today's conflicts are often very widely and broadly scattered, often without patterns, which makes it extremely difficult to do clearance," Katrin Atkins, research lead at Landmine Monitor, a global mine monitoring initiative, told the BBC.

The dangers are reflected in a sharp rise in casualties across Syria.

A total of 1,602 people were killed or injured by landmines and explosive remnants of war in 2025 alone, an increase of more than 50% on the previous year, according to the Landmine Monitor report, published by the International Campaign to Ban Landmines on Tuesday.

Only Myanmar, with 1,891 casualties, recorded more.

The report attributes the rise in Syria partly to the large-scale return of refugees and internally displaced people following Assad's overthrow.

"When you look at population movement, a couple of months later you see an increase of incidents in these areas, because that's exactly where people go and start to use the fields again," Atkins said.

More than 60% of casualties recorded by the world's largest landmine clearance charity, the Halo Trust, involve farming, agriculture or shepherding, according to its Syria programme manager, Simon Jackson.

"They're not going to wait for the government or for organisations like us to come and tell them that everything is safe and clear," he told the BBC. "People are taking risks out of desperation."

Since Assad's fall on 8 December 2024, Unmas has recorded at least 1,556 incidents in Syria involving explosive ordnance.

They have caused 2,701 casualties: 935 people killed and 1,766 injured. Children account for 995 of those victims, or about 37%.

But the toll is likely to be higher, with experts estimating that overall casualties could be under-reported by 25-50%.

Both government forces and rebels laid land mines during the civil war

For many of those who survive, the consequences are devastating and permanent.

Majid Abdul Rahman, 27, was travelling in a car in the Ghouta region on the outskirts of Damascus in March 2025 when it struck an explosive device. The vehicle filled with smoke and he felt a pain in his leg.

More than a year later, Majid has difficulty walking and has been unable to return to work, leaving him struggling to provide for his family.

The danger has continued into this month, with at least 20 people killed and more than 40 injured by explosive ordnance in November.

In one of the worst recorded incidents, an explosion in a school courtyard in Hasakah on 4 November killed a 12-year-old and injured 19 others. And on Monday, a 13-year-old boy was killed when an unexploded cluster munition detonated in southern Daraa province.

The threat is also holding back Syria's economic recovery. Contaminated farmland cannot be safely cultivated, while people collecting scrap metal or trying to salvage materials from abandoned areas also risk triggering explosions.

The Syrian Ministry of Defence says since the end of 2024 its teams have found and dealt with more than 315,000 mines and unexploded munitions.

But no nationwide survey has established how much land remains contaminated, making it difficult to assess the full scale of the problem.

"We're in a bit of a black hole," Atkins said.

And with the extent of contamination still largely unknown, the war continues to claim victims two years after it ended.

Additional reporting by Lana Antaki in Damascus
