#55. US jury convicts Huawei of violating sanctions, stealing trade secrets

Huawei was found guilty of 10 out of 12 charges levelled against it. The company said it plans to appeal the verdict.

A US jury convicted Chinese tech giant Huawei on a slew of charges on Tuesday including stealing trade secrets and violating sanctions.

The jury in the Brooklyn federal court found the company guilty on 10 of the 12 criminal counts that it faced, which also included bank fraud and wire fraud.

"For decades, Huawei and three of its subsidiaries conducted their business in the United States on the basis of lies and theft," US Attorney General Todd Blanche said after the verdict.

However, the jury acquitted Huawei on two charges of racketeering and conspiring to obstruct justice.

The verdict is the latest blow to the Chinese tech giant which has for years been banned from selling networking devices to telecom carries in the US and other Western countries. However, its consumer electronics business has continued to grow as its networking business in other countries.

Prosecutors detailed a number of ways Huawei stole trade secrets from competitors in order to undercut them in the market.

In once instance, a Huawei employee slipped into a competitor's booth at a Chicago trade fair in order to photograph circuitboards inside another company's networking divice.

Huawei was also found to have once sold routers containing source code from a rival company.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Prosecutors also said a Huawei employee once removed a robotic arm from a competitor's lab so it could be photographed and measured.

According to court records, the victim companies included Cisco Systems, Motorola Solutions, T-Mobile, Quintel USA and Fujitsu.

"The misappropriated intellectual property included trade secret information and copyrighted works, such as source code and user manuals for internet routers, antenna technology, and robot testing technology," the Department of Justice said.

Prosecutors also alleged that Huawei did business in countries subject to US sanctions including Iran and North Korea.

Huawei then made false statements to both US government officials and banks in order to hide this activity, prosecutors said.

"Few crimes are as serious as Huawei's decades-long pattern of endangering US national security, violating the sanctity of US commerce, and impeding the ‌enforcement of ​US laws," US Attorney Joseph Nocella said.

Huawei continued to deny the allegations even after the verdict on Tuesday.

"We believe the government's remaining charges are equally without legal or factual merit," the company said in a statement.

"The five trade secret-related disputes cited by the government were all civil matters that were resolved over the past two decades. No court ever found Huawei liable for willful intellectual property misappropriation, and the company was never ordered to pay damages for trade secret misappropriation."

The company's lawyer, Brian Heberlig, said: "We fully expect to appeal."

Huawei is set to be sentenced next September and could face billions of dollars in penalties.

#54. Indian government has 'lost the plot', says 'cockroach' leader barred from UK flight

Watch: India's government is 'scared', cockroach movement leader says after travel ban

One of the leaders of India's "cockroach" movement has said the Delhi government has "completely lost the plot" after he was stopped from leaving the country for the UK.

Saurav Das said he was meant to board a flight on Tuesday morning but was told by immigration officers his passport had been "flagged" because of discrepancies involving his date of birth.

In an interview with the BBC, Das said there were no discrepancies in his documents, accusing the Indian government of using cheap tactics to stop activists from travelling and speaking on issues that matter to young people.

The BBC has reached out to the federal home ministry, Delhi police and airport officials for comment.

Saurav told the BBC World Service's Newshour programme the incident showed the government was "scared".

"They are scared of the cockroach movement. They're scared of young people asking them questions because they have never had to deal with a generation that wants to ask questions so head on. So that jitters them."

By not allowing him to travel, he said the government had lowered India's stature in the world.

"It's international news that I am unable to travel to the UK," he said. "And this shows Prime Minister Modi in such poor light.

"It's a way to harass, to target activists, young voices in India by the Modi government."

Das said he had been invited by the Oxford Union, as well as universities including Cambridge and Edinburgh, to speak about the movement.

Ratna Singh, another leader of the Cockroach Janta Party (CJP), wrote: "The manner in which Saurav was not allowed to board today raises deeply troubling questions about the state of freedom of expression and the space being allowed for young voices in this country."

On Tuesday, the London School of Economics Student Union's Amnesty International Unit, a student-led charity group, shared a post on Instagram saying that an event featuring Das had been "indefinitely postponed".

Saurav Das (L) speaking following the resignation of India's education minister in July

Das, a former journalist, became one of the faces of the CJP - a Gen Z-led movement that began as satire but has since emerged as a youth-led pressure group.

In July, protests spearheaded by the CJP over exam paper leaks led to the resignation of India's Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan .

In October, CJP leaders including Das were among thousands detained by the police for trying to hold protests demanding the resignation of India's Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar over alleged irregularities in electoral rolls. Kumar and the Election Commission have denied wrongdoing.

The same month, Das and Ashutosh Ranka, another CJP leader, were jailed for seven days after being arrested for trespassing at a banned protest site in Delhi.

Das insisted the CJP would continue protesting, but it did not want to be a political party "as of today".

"It's a long haul. We are not here for a short time. We are looking at India in the next 10 to 20 years. How do we develop India into a better country? That is the vision that we want to carry forward."

Abhijeet Dipke, the founder of CJP, wrote on X that if Das "had looted a bank or committed financial fraud", he may have been allowed to fly to London, referring to billionaire bank-loan defaulters who have left India.

There have been other instances in recent years of people being prevented from boarding flights.

In September 2025, Bhanu Tatak, an environmental and anti-dam activist from India's north-eastern Arunachal Pradesh state, was stopped from boarding a flight to Dublin.

In March 2022, Rana Ayyub, an investigative journalist and outspoken critic of Prime Minister Narendra Modi's government, was also stopped from boarding a flight to London at the Mumbai airport. She was due to speak at different events on media freedom and the targeting of journalists.

Additional reporting by Cherylann Mollan.

#50. China-linked network using AI fakes to smear Taiwan election candidates, BBC Verify finds

Taiwan's ruling party is being repeatedly targeted with AI videos spreading false claims about its leader and other key members ahead of crucial local elections, BBC Verify has found.

A China-linked network has pumped out more than 500 fake videos about the Democratic Progressive Party (DPP) in the past year, which experts suggest is part of a long-running campaign to influence the outcome of the elections.

The videos appear to try to smear President Lai Ching-te by claiming he holds a US green card - and would abandon Taiwan if war broke out with China - which Taiwan's presidential office says is completely false. Deepfakes also appear to show Puma Shen, who is running to be the mayor of capital Taipei, using drugs, which he denies. Others seek to undermine or ridicule the politicians by showing them in children's clothing or as popular film characters.

The Taipei mayoralty has traditionally served as a springboard to the presidency. Experts say this has made Shen, who has been openly critical of Beijing, an obvious target for disinformation campaigns. The current mayor, Chiang Wan-an, is part of Taiwan's Kuomintang (KMT) party, which favours closer ties with China.

Taiwan is a self-governing island that China claims as part of its territory. Beijing and Taipei have long observed an uneasy status quo under which Taiwan, backed by the US, neither formally declares independence nor unites with China.

But tensions have risen since the DPP came to power in 2016. Beijing has repeatedly accused the party of pursuing independence while China's leader Xi Jinping, who has said "reunification" with Taiwan is a key goal, has stepped up pressure against the party with increased military activity around the island.

Experts believe the current disinformation activity is part of a broader campaign against Taiwan designed to undermine trust in its democratic process.

The campaign has targeted Taiwan's President Lai (top) and his predecessor Tsai (bottom)

"The account operators are trying to make people distrust the DPP politicians so that they will not believe whatever they say," Austin Horng-En Wang, an expert in cognitive warfare at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas, told BBC Verify. "If you don't trust that candidate then you will not listen to what they say," he added.

The videos, many of which were shared from an account called "noxparable" or carried a watermark with the same name, have been viewed tens of millions of times across several social media platforms since May 2025.

More than half of those views were tracked in the past four months as the activity has ramped up ahead of the elections. Many of the accounts involved in spreading disinformation listed their locations on social media as mainland China and Hong Kong, while a number of the clips carried labels showing they were generated with AI. Many people who engaged with the videos were often seen repeating the false claims or attacking DPP figures in the comments, while others appeared to treat the clips as entertainment.

One of the most widely shared videos targeted Shen by superimposing his face onto a man who is seen holding a lighter and exhaling smoke alongside claims he is smoking drugs. The video appears to build on accusations from Taiwan's opposition party that he supports drugs decriminalisation, which Shen denies, and was debunked by a local news network.

Shen has taken legal action against people sharing deepfakes, local media reported, while his campaign office said AI videos showing the candidate appeared to be part of a "classic tactic China uses to smear specific targets". BBC Verify contacted the Chinese embassy in London for comment.

Many of the fake videos ridiculed President Lai and Shen while others portrayed other KMT candidates such as Ko Chih-en (above) in a positive light

Other debunked claims include allegations that Lai's predecessor Tsai Ing-wen had fraudulently obtained her PhD from the London School of Economics. The university pushed back on identical allegations in 2019 and confirmed it had awarded her the doctorate.

A number of the social media accounts BBC Verify identified as sharing the disinformation videos appeared to be linked to Chinese firm Borderless, which produces websites that blend apolitical content with pro-China messaging. The network has also been investigated by journalists at the Taiwanese independent media outlet The Reporter, who shared their findings with BBC Verify. Borderless has previously been accused of carrying out "cognitive warfare" against Taiwan by Taipei's National Security Bureau and the firm has been contacted for comment.

Analysts confirmed to BBC Verify there were also linguistic clues that the accounts, which presented themselves as based in Taiwan, were actually posting from China. The posts occasionally used a simplified Chinese script introduced by the Communist government which was never adopted in Taiwan.

The posts mostly appeared during regular office hours, which Wang suggested showed a "professional" operation, while the accounts originating outside Taiwan showed evidence of election interfering. "When they pretended to be citizens in another country and used these accounts to manipulate the public opinion of the other country during the campaign, it is definitely election interference," Wang added.

Earlier this month, Taiwan's central election commission aired an advert warning voters to "beware of AI-generated deepfake videos" circulating online that could seek to influence the upcoming election. It comes as government officials flagged nearly 2,700 social media accounts to Meta that it claimed were part of a Chinese information warfare campaign, Reuters reported. Meta said it had removed the accounts.

Andrew Yeh of the China Strategic Risk Institute said the ultimate goal is not to favour any party. "China's information operations succeed not just by backing one candidate or opposing another, but by diluting Taiwan's policy debates, driving polarisation and degrading Taiwanese citizens' trust in their democratic processes," he said.

"Regardless of whether the KMT or DPP comes out on top, a more fractured and more divided Taiwan is in Beijing's interests."

What do you want BBC Verify to investigate?

#48. China launches Shenzhou-24 to Tiangong station

A new Chinese crew is on its way to the Tiangong station after a launch that China called a complete success. This includes a Pakistani pilot, who is making a brief visit.

The Shenzhou-24 spacecraft lifted off on a Long March-2F rocket at 8:18 am local time (0018 GMT) from the Jiuquan launch center in the Gobi Desert.

The China Manned Space Agency reported that the capsule separated from the rocket about 10 minutes later and entered orbit, and called the launch a "complete success".

State broadcaster CCTV showed the three crew members leafing through a manual minutes after liftoff and reported that they were in good condition.

Tang Hongbo is making his third trip to space. CCTV said he will become the first astronaut to board Tiangong three times. Wu Yi is on his first flight, as is Pakistani pilot Muhammad Zeeshan Ali, the first foreigner to fly on a Chinese crewed mission.

"I carry the name of Pakistan on my shoulder, the hopes of 250 million Pakistanis," Ali told a news conference on Tuesday, switching between Urdu, Mandarin and English.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The handover on the station will be unusual. Shenzhou-23 commander Zhu Yangzhu will remain on board for a year, while the rest of his crew — Lai Ka-ying, Hong Kong's first astronaut, and Zhang Zhiyuan — return to Earth with Ali.

Zhu's stay is reportedly China's first full-year spell in space. Reuters said it will be the longest single stay by a Chinese astronaut. It has been described as a test of how the human body copes, ahead of planned Moon missions and possibly a journey to Mars.

The crew will carry out more than 90 experiments and technology tests, Xinhua reported. At a news conference on Tuesday, Tang said differences in language, culture and psychology had made teamwork harder, but that both sides' efforts were paying off.

Ali, speaking in Mandarin, told reporters: "It's like my daily training. Day after day, reaching new heights."

China's 2030 target for a crewed Moon landing is unchanged despite the delay to its Chang'e-7 lunar mission. The United States is aiming for 2028.

#45. Israeli air strike kills Hamas internal security chief in Gaza

A funeral was held earlier in southern Gaza for three Palestinians killed in an Israeli strike

At least seven Palestinians, including a senior Hamas security official, have been killed in Israeli air strikes in the Gaza Strip, health officials and first responders say.

Naeem Abu Nahel, the head of Hamas's internal security service, was targeted in western Gaza City, Gaza's Hamas-run interior ministry said. The 50-year-old had only been in the position since September, when his predecessor was captured in a raid by Israeli special forces.

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and Defence Minister Israel Katz announced that Abu Nahel was "eliminated" by the Israeli military.

The six other people were killed in two earlier air strikes elsewhere in Gaza.

Three people, including a teenage boy, were killed in the Wadi Gaza area, south of Gaza City, according to the health officials and first responders. The other three were killed at a police station in the al-Mawasi area, west of Khan Younis, they added.

Gaza's police force, which operates under the interior ministry, said two of its officers were among those who died in the second attack. It also accused Israel of "an ongoing campaign targeting the police apparatus aimed at sowing chaos" in the territory.

The Israeli military said it struck "military operatives" in both areas.

Witnesses said an Israeli drone targeted Naeem Abu Nahel as he was walking on a side street near a tent camp for displaced people in Gaza City.

Several other people were reportedly seriously injured in the strike.

The Israeli military said Abu Nahel had "possessed extensive institutional knowledge" about Hamas's internal security service - which is responsible for enforcing discipline, protecting infrastructure and rooting out informants - and that his death constituted "a blow to the apparatus's management capabilities".

It also alleged that he had advanced attacks on Israeli troops and civilians during the war and that it killed him "to remove the threat he posed".

Gaza's interior ministry said Abu Nahel's killing followed what it described as the internal security service's "important achievements in combating gangs collaborating with the occupation [Israel] and operating in areas under the control of its military".

Gaza's health ministry warned that there was a rapid rise in cases of meningitis in October

Violence has continued in Gaza since Israel and Hamas agreed to a US-brokered ceasefire in October last year. Israeli strikes take place almost daily in areas under Hamas's control, which are home to 2.1 million people, most of them displaced.

At least 1,679 people have been killed in Gaza over the past 13 months, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza during the same period.

The humanitarian situation also remains dire, with heavy rainfall earlier this month causing flooding and damage to thousands of shelters for displaced families.

The UN and its partners have called on Israel - which controls Gaza's crossings - to allow in more supplies to help Palestinians prepare for the winter, including tents, heaters and water pumps, as well as vaccines and medicines to combat infectious diseases.

On Tuesday, the health ministry warned that there had been a rapid rise in cases of meningitis, with 272 reported in October - about eight times the usual monthly average.

Fifteen deaths had been attributed to the infection so far this year, including five reported between August and October, it said.

Israeli military body Cogat said on Monday it was "working to provide a dedicated humanitarian response" and that a newly approved package of pumps, generators, water tankers and medical equipment had been presented at a meeting with UN agencies.

The Gaza war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,740 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#43. Libya: 2 dead after Ukrainian attack on Russian warship in Benghazi

The head of the eastern administration in Libya has confirmed that two Libyan soldiers succumbed to their injuries. Ukraine's foreign minister defended the attack as an effort to counter Russian aggression.

Two Libyan soldiers have died from their injuries sustained during a Ukrainian attack on a Russian warship in docked in the port of Benghazi.

Libya is currently divided between a UN-recognized government in Tripoli and a parallel administration in the east of the country that controls Benghazi.

The head of the eastern administration, Oussama Hamad, said the two men had died "as a result of their injuries during the treacherous Ukrainian aggression against the port of Benghazi."

The Libyan National Army General Command said a response would come "at the appropriate time and place."

Earlier, on Monday, Libya's Foreign ministry denounced the attacks and said it didn't authorize "any foreign military operations within [Libya's] territory or territorial waters.

The naval drone attack that occurred on Sunday was claimed by Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on Monday. On Tuesday, Ukraine's Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha defended the attack.

"Ukraine is not 'dragging' anyone into the war or 'transferring' the war anywhere," Sybiha said on Telegram.

"Ukraine respects the sovereignty and territorial integrity of Libya. We emphasize that Ukraine's actions are aimed at countering Russian armed aggression and are not intended ‌to harm Libya or its people."

He said Russia was the one endangering third countries by using their civilian infrastrure for military purposes.

According to the Institute for the Study of War, the Russian corvette warship had stopped for several days in Benghazi's port to refuel and resupply.

Another Ukrainian naval drone attack on Sunday sank a Russian-owned container ship that was allegedly transporting military equipment.

Ukraine has in recent months sought to attack Russian targets further and further away, including a series of strikes in Siberia.

#41. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#38. These North Koreans say they were spied on, beaten and jailed for being Christian

Kim Jong Un's communist regime is accused of brutal crackdowns on religious freedom in a new report

Living in North Korea, Kim Bo-bin got used to praying with her eyes open.

She found ingenious ways to keep her Christian faith a secret in the Communist dictatorship, which tightly controls how and where its citizens worship. Unauthorised displays of faith could land people in prison, she tells the BBC.

So Kim would silently mouth hymns and clap with just two of her fingers. "In North Korea, if there are three people in a room, you are being spied on. So I would only close my eyes [and pray] when I lay on the floor."

Now 44, Kim lives just outside the South Korean capital Seoul, where she fled 15 years ago.

Her testimony - of how she prayed in secret for years, and was jailed and beaten for her faith - is part of a new report documenting the experiences of 570 North Korean escapees, most of whom say they experienced or witnessed anti-Christian persecution.

Spanning five years and featuring interviews with defectors from across the country, the report by British human rights group Korea Future accuses Kim Jong Un's regime of repeatedly violating religious freedom through a system of surveillance, fear and violence. It suggests little has changed after sustained international pressure, with multiple incidents of suspected Christians being beaten and tortured as recently as last year.

North Korea maintains that it allows religious freedom in spite of years of evidence heard by rights groups and the United Nations. While churches exist in Pyongyang, they are controlled by the state and widely seen as projecting an illusion of religious liberty.

"The North Korean leader's authority is god-like," says Kang Dong-wan, professor at Dong-a University. "So they cannot allow gods whose authority is above him." Pyongyang fears that religious freedom may threaten the regime itself, he adds.

Kim Bo-bin has been living in South Korea for 15 years now

First established in Korea in the late 1700s, Christianity exploded across the north after Protestant missionaries arrived in the 1880s. North Korea provides no figures on its Christian population but the UN has referred to estimates of 200,000 to 400,000 of them practising secretly, some of whom are detained in camps.

Surveillance and control over religion, like many other aspects of North Korean life, intensified under Kim Jong Un, who succeeded his father in 2011.

Three South Korean missionaries operating in the Chinese border region were arrested between 2013-2014 and sentenced to life in prison on charges of espionage and plotting a coup. It's not clear if they are still alive, as Seoul says Pyongyang has refused to share any information about the detainees.

Pyongyang has also been cracking down on smuggled memory sticks containing South Korean-made videos of Christian sermons. In 2020, it passed a law - on Rejecting Reactionary Ideology and Culture - to block so-called "foreign influence". Owning Bibles and hosting services can now result in imprisonment, while distributing religious books or videos is punishable by death.

Lessons against religion begin as early as kindergarten for most North Koreans. Children must attend "self-criticism" sessions at school in which they are warned against "superstitions", widely understood to refer to Christianity and other faiths.

An image from 2010 showing the inside of a state-sanctioned church in Pyongyang

This is what Kim Bo-bin recalls being taught. The faiths Koreans have long practised - Christianity since the 18th Century and shamanism, a millennia-old practice rooted in ancestral spirit worship - were dismissed as superstitions.

Her teachers never mentioned that Kim Il Sung himself was born into a fervently Christian family, or that Pyongyang had once been dubbed the "Jerusalem of the East."

Rather, every child and adult is expected to know North Korea's foundational Ten Principles by heart. They state that citizens must hold the "firm perspective" they know of "nothing greater than the great comrades" Kim Il Sung, the founder of North Korea, his son Kim Jong Il and the ruling party. The "smallest attempt" to dent this authority must not be tolerated, it adds.

The report documents both the indoctrination as well as the constant surveillance, with police and a network of neighbourhood spies monitoring suspicious activities. A former police informant says she was instructed to gather information on neighbours' worshipping, and handed a memo by an officer describing Christianity as an "anti-socialist activity".

North Korean authorities are also reported to hold intergenerational records of families' beliefs condemning Christians' children and grandchildren to a life without higher education or decent career prospects.

All of this illustrates how various North Korean institutions, including schools and the police, control its citizens' beliefs from "cradle to the grave," co-author James Burt says.

And yet many North Koreans turn to religion because their lives are so precarious, he adds. Faith offers them something that the state ideology "with its rigid doctrines and disconnect from their lived realities" cannot.

North Koreans pay tribute to statues of the late Kim Il Sung and Kim Jong Il

Kim Bo-bin grew up in North Hamgyong province, along the border with China. It was a remote, impoverished part of the country where those deemed "untrustworthy" by the state ended up, such as landowners and Christians.

Stuck at the bottom of a state-enforced hierarchy, this "hostile class" had little chance of moving up the ladder. Many of Kim's neighbours were barred from higher education and government positions, or ever living in Pyongyang.

One of them was an exiled "crazy grandma" who used to disappear into a coal shed every morning to chant words 10-year-old Kim didn't recognise. "One day, [the grandma] told me we go from ashes to ashes and dust to dust," she recalls. It was only 12 years later, when she encountered a Bible for the first time in her life that she realised the grandma was a Christian.

When a devastating famine began in the late 1990s, North Hamgyong was one of the first regions to starve because it was cut off from the state's food supply which prioritised Pyongyang. Millions were killed in what the regime described euphemistically as the "Arduous March". It was a time when many North Koreans crossed into China seeking food and work.

Kim was one of them. Aged 22 in 2003, she took what she believed was a job opportunity in China offered by a friend's aunt. But once she crossed the border, she was forced into marriage with a Chinese man and became pregnant.

While picking up a free pram for her newborn daughter at a building adorned with a cross, she encountered a missionary for the first time. Kim would join a growing group of people who discovered Christianity in China - a group that Pyongyang saw as a threat when they were returned to the North, according to Prof Kang.

A view of the city of Chongjin in North Korea's North Hamgyong province

Kim was arrested in China in 2007 and deported. During interrogation, she says North Korean secret police asked her if she'd ever worn a cross on her neck. She pretended not to understand. She told the police she only recognised the symbol from hospital buildings.

Kim Bo-bin says it was God's blessing the interrogator seemed more interested in whether she intended to flee to South Korea, a grave offence. She says she was beaten for 23 days and sentenced to three years of hard labour at a prison. Her story tallies with multiple accounts in Korea Future's report of extreme police violence causing broken bones and severe bleeding.

Lee Young-joo, who Kim Bo-bin met in prison also after being deported from China, tells the BBC she was interrogated about whether she'd met any missionaries, been to a church or seen a Bible. "Of course you must deny all of it. I said no, I haven't seen it," she told them.

In her prison cell, Lee says she'd keep her eyes open, begging God to save her life. Whenever anyone shut their eyes, guards would swear and shout: "Are you praying?"

Lee is glad she wasn't found to be a Christian, because she says she "would have been sent to a political prisoners' camp. I would be tortured and never allowed to leave and be buried there." This is a widely-held fear among defectors, although Korea Future's report did not hear any first-hand testimonies of Christians being held in such camps.

Lee, who previously shared with the BBC the abuse she suffered inside prison, says she was sentenced to three-and-half years. Outside of hard labour, she would spend time praying, sometimes with other Christian inmates. They would hold hands and whisper Bible verses from memory.

Lee Young-joo first met a Christian after she escaped from North Korea into China

She and Kim Bo-bin eventually returned home after serving their sentences, where they continued to practise their religion in secret, they tell the BBC.

Kim says she managed to convert her younger sister and other relatives: "My aunt's walls are thick. The neighbours can't hear us so I quietly taught them hymns and about the all-knowing and all-powerful God."

Lee tells the BBC her mother was horrified when she learned of her Christian faith. Undeterred, she told her that she would escape again to join her daughter, who had been left in China as Lee was arrested and deported. When her mother protested, she responded saying they could all live together later.

Her incredulous mother asked: where? "In heaven," she replied.

Today, Lee attends a small church of North Korean escapees in Seoul. She prays daily, to thank God for the chicken soup and the stroll along the river. She prays for her daughter, who escaped with her in 2010, to find a nice Christian man to marry. Her mother died in North Korea some years after her escape, she was told.

Kim managed to flee to South Korea eventually, in 2012, at the age of 30, along with her daughter. She prays throughout the day - and not silently. At church on Sundays, she is the loudest singer.

Could Kim's teen daughter become North Korea's next leader?

#35. India news: US prepares new tariffs after trade probe

The United States is reportedly readying fresh tariffs on India and 15 other economies under a trade probe. Also, a woman was allegedly gangraped by her colleagues in the national capital. Follow live.

Here are the biggest headlines from India on Wednesday, November 25:

Three people have been arrested after a 22-year-old woman said she was gang-raped by two men, with assistance from others, at a cafe in east Delhi, local media said citing police sources.

Police were currently making efforts to trace three others who remained in hiding.

News agency Press Trust of India reported that the incident took place when the victim gathered at the cafe with six colleagues on Monday night for a party. The cafe had shut for the day before midnight on Monday but the group entered after hours as they had the keys to the establishment. News channel NDTV reported that the victim and her colleagues worked at the cafe.

Delhi Police has launched a formal investigation against four men and two women under India's criminal law. Three suspects, including at least one woman, have been arrested so far.

This comes just less than two months after a woman was gang-raped in a public park in Delhi, triggering public anger and protests. Women's safety remains a contentious issue in India with frequent headlines of violence against women in the news.

India could be one of the 16 countries facing fresh and imminent US tariffs with the Trump administration set to release the findings of a probe aimed at countering alleged excess industrial capacities, Reuters reported citing three people with knowledge of the matter.

The results of the investigation are likely to be released over the course of the day, followed by new import duties on a wide range of goods if the probe find India guilty of excess capacity.

It remains unclear what tariff rates would be proposed but one of the sources told the news agency that they would be specifically tailored to each country.

That said, the US is likely to honor capped tariff rates agreed on in finalized US trade deals with partner countries.

A report by the Wall Street Journal said that India could face higher US tariffs than economies like Japan or the European Union.

The US introduced 25% reciprocal tariffs in 2025 on India, under US President Donald Trump's second term. The rate was later lowered to 18% with Washington and New Delhi agreeing to an interim trade framework while a more comprehensive trade agreement would be worked on. Leaders from both nations have since said the first tranche of a trade agreement was "close," but a deal remains elusive.

Meanwhile in July 2026, the United States Trade Representative (USTR), which carries out trade-related investigations, imposed an additional 10% tariffs on India and other countries after a probe on traded goods made using forced labor.

Good morning! and welcome to DW's India blog.

This is Mahima Kapoor reporting from Studio Delhi and I will be your guide through India's news cycle.

The national capital once again woke up to the news of an alleged gang-rape in east Delhi where a woman was assaulted by her colleagues on Monday night. Local media reports say at least one of the three held is a woman, while three others remain absconding.

In other news, India is among a bunch of countries that might face fresh US tariffs under a trade investigation. US President Donald Trump's administration has been using trade probes as a proxy to reciprocal tariffs. The results of the latest probe are expected soon.

He took a commercial flight to the country on Tuesday.

Ãbrego GarcÃ­a, who is married to a US citizen and lived in Maryland, came to the US illegally from El Salvador as a teenager.

#32. Domestic violence soars to record high in Germany

Every three days in Germany, a man kills his wife or ex-partner. Every 10 minutes, the police record a sexual offense against a woman. "A shocking finding," according to Justice Minister Stefanie Hubig.

The German government has presented some grim statistics with regard to misogyny, rape, violence and murder, with reported cases of domestic violence at an all-time high.

At a press conference in Berlin on Tuesday, Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt, Family Affairs Minister Karin Prien and Justice Minister Stefanie Hubig released the latest figures on crimes that specifically target women: whether within families and intimate relationships, in public or online.

In contrast to the statistics pertaining to crime overall, which have remained the same or increased only slightly, reports of crimes against women have increased.

"Behind all these terrible numbers lie individual stories, people and particularly women and girls, whose lives are severely affected by these acts of violence," said Dobrindt.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In 2025, some 280,000 people were victims of physical abuse in their own homes, a 2.8% increase compared to the previous year. Over 70% of the victims were female. Each hour, 16 women are subjected to physical abuse behind closed doors.

The male victims included boys who had been assaulted by their fathers.

The number of misogynistic hate crimes has also risen dramatically, by almost 50%, to 819 cases.

"The figures show us what it can mean to be a woman in Germany," said Hubig. "It can mean being killed by one's partner or ex-partner. Being a woman can also mean being physically abused by one's partner or ex-partner in one's own home. Being a woman can mean that one's sexual autonomy is brutally disregarded."

According to the latest findings, both perpetrators and victims are getting younger. In 2025, nearly half of all female victims of sexual offenses were under the age of 18. And the number of incidents of online violence against women and girls rose by 15.7%. This includes the unauthorized sharing of videos and photos, verbal abuse, manipulation of images, threats and online blackmail, as well as cyberstalking.

Because many perpetrators and victims are still in their teens, Prien, who is also minister for education, said the government wanted to focus primarily on schools, which she explained should become places of prevention.

"Ultimately, we need to bring about a change in people's mindsets, especially those of men. That will be the deciding factor in the end," she said.

Over the weekend, Justice Minister Hubig had already announced that in future all femicides, the intentional killing of girls and women because of their gender, would be classified as murders. Currently, such crimes are often classified as manslaughter, for which the penalty is less severe than for murder.

Hubig told German broadcaster ZDF that it was increasingly common that femicides be described as family tragedies or as domestic dramas revolving around jealousy and then classified as manslaughter.

Martina Link, the vice president of the Federal Criminal Police Office, was also at the press conference in Berlin. She called for everybody to pay closer attention to the issue, within their own families, neighborhoods or at work.

"Violence against women is not a private matter," she said. "Not in our immediate surroundings nor in the digital world."

She also pointed out that when it came to sexual offenses and domestic violence, only about 5-10% of cases were reported.

This article was originally written in German.

The 82-year-old has denied possessing images of child sex abuse.

The FBI arrested Markle on 6 November at an elderly care home in Florida on a single federal charge of possessing child sexual abuse material. He now faces two counts.

US attorney Bill Essayli said Markle faces a maximum of 20 years in prison on each count if convicted.

His arrest came after a search of a storage unit he had allegedly rented in Los Angeles for almost two decades uncovered the abuse material.

Watch: Thomas Markle's charges over child sexual abuse material

#23. The rising cost of putting food on the table in India

As food inflation rises, vegetables are becoming an increasingly expensive part of the household budget

India's inflation is rising again. And the biggest worry is what's happening to food prices.

The latest government data, external shows retail inflation climbed to 6.09% in October, up from 5.54% in September.

But look more closely at food inflation. It has surged to 8.94%.

The squeeze was sharper in rural India, where food inflation was 9.09%, compared with 8.68% in urban areas.

In other words, food prices are rising considerably faster than the overall rate of inflation.

Food inflation this year has been driven by a combination of weather disruptions, supply shortages and rising input costs, say economists.

With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, external, India remains acutely vulnerable to the vagaries of the monsoon. This year, the southwest monsoon was 13% below normal and unevenly distributed, while intensifying El NiÃ±o conditions have heightened risks to farm production.

Onion prices have surged, adding to the pressure on household food budgets

A survey by polling agency LocalCircles found that six in 10 consumers say household essentials cost them over 20% more each month than a year ago, prompting calls for government intervention.

Rising prices are also forcing cutbacks: 74% are curbing discretionary spending on eating out, shopping and entertainment, while half are buying smaller quantities of essentials.

Food accounts for a smaller share of Indian household spending than it once did, but remains the biggest driver of inflation, with significant economic, social and political consequences.

In India's latest consumer price index, food and beverages account for 36.75% of the basket of goods and services used to measure inflation.

That is down sharply from 45.86% under the previous series, reflecting changing consumption patterns as Indians spend more on services, transport, healthcare, education and other non-food items.

Yet a sharp increase in food prices can move the overall inflation rate significantly, while disproportionately affecting people whose incomes leave little room for adjustment.

"Adjustability for a poor country like India is significantly lower," says Rajeswari Sengupta, an associate professor of economics at Mumbai-based Indira Gandhi Institute of Development Research, pointing to the fact that a substantial proportion of household income is still spent on food.

Rising food prices hurt the poor the most

According to calculations by Dharmakirti Joshi, chief economist at analytics firm Crisil, the bottom 20% of rural households faced inflation of 6.9% in October, against 6.5% for the richest 20%.

In urban India, the corresponding rates were 6.1% and 5.3%.

The pressure is visible even in the cost of a simple meal.

Crisil's Roti Rice Rate analysis, external found that the cost of a home-cooked vegetarian thali - a meal platter - rose 12% year-on-year in October, driven by higher prices of onions, tomatoes, vegetables, edible oil, rice and cooking gas. A non-vegetarian thali became 9% more expensive.

Rice prices were up about 10% year-on-year, pulses 8%, edible oil 13% and LPG 10%, according to Pushan Sharma, director of intelligence at Crisil. "It's no longer a few commodities that are driving this increase, it's becoming a little more broad-based," he says.

When onion prices spike, households can cut back on their consumption to some extent. But substituting rice, pulses and cooking oil is considerably harder.

And food inflation is not just about what farmers produce. It is also about how efficiently that food is stored, processed and moved to the market. Weaknesses at each stage can turn seasonal shortages into sharp price spikes - and gluts into waste.

Onionomics: Peeling away the layers of India's food economy

Onions, for instance, are particularly susceptible to seasonal price swings.

The rabi crop - winter crops, sown after the monsoon and harvested in spring - which accounts for roughly two-thirds of onion production, is harvested in March and April and stored for several months. As stocks dwindle, prices can rise sharply before the next crop arrives.

Tomatoes, meanwhile, tend to see price spikes during the summer months, when arrivals decline in some markets or heat stress affects production.

Sharma says perishables are often responsible for the sudden spikes in food inflation, while prices of staples such as rice and wheat tend to rise more gradually but can stay higher for longer.

"The perishables are what caused the volatility. The non-perishables, like rice and wheat, are the ones that structurally increase the cost," he says.

Economists say India still lacks adequate infrastructure to sort, grade, store and process much of its perishable produce.

Tomatoes, for example, can become so abundant during a glut that farmers struggle to sell them at remunerative prices, only for consumers to face sharply higher prices months later.

With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, India remains vulnerable to erratic monsoons

Better storage and processing could help turn some of that surplus into products such as puree, smoothing the extremes between abundance and scarcity, according to Sharma

Energy shocks feed into the cost of transport, irrigation and agricultural inputs. Fertiliser prices can rise. Global edible-oil markets matter because India relies heavily on imports.

To be true, India, however, has some protection against the worst effects of food inflation.

Government stocks of cereals are comfortably above prescribed minimum levels, and the free-food programme covering nearly 800 million people offers a cushion against rising prices. Rice stocks, external are particularly ample.

There is a wider economic risk, says Sengupta.

"Food inflation may begin with a supply shock, but its effects can spread. Workers who find that their wages no longer cover basic living costs may seek higher pay. Businesses facing higher labour costs may raise prices. What begins as a rise in the price of food can gradually become a more general inflationary pressure - the familiar wage-price spiral that central banks try to prevent," she says.

For India, this creates a particularly difficult balancing act.

Agriculture is a relatively low-productivity sector, and the government has long sought to protect farmers by setting minimum support prices (MSPs) for certain crops - a price floor at which it promises to buy produce, helping farmers guard against a market crash.

At the same time, food prices are politically sensitive. Sharp increases in the price of staples such as onions and tomatoes can quickly anger consumers, prompting governments to step in with measures such as restricting exports, limiting how much traders can stockpile, or otherwise trying to bring prices down.

The broader story of India's changing food consumption makes the current inflation more interesting.

Households are eating differently from a decade ago. An analysis, external of the 2011-12 and 2023-24 consumption surveys shows that the share of cereals in food spending has fallen sharply, particularly among the poorest households, reflecting the impact of subsidised food programmes.

Meanwhile, consumption of nutrient-rich foods such as dairy, fruit and meat has increased across income groups, with the biggest gains among the poorest, the study revealed.

India's food basket is becoming more diverse. But diversity is meaningful only if ordinary Indians can afford it. Ultimately, food inflation is about what makes it to the plate - and what has to be left out.

#20. Huawei found guilty of stealing US trade secrets but acquitted of racketeering

Defence lawyer Brian Heberlig makes closing arguments at the Huawei Technologies trial at Brooklyn federal court on November 17.

Huawei has been found guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets and bank fraud, but not guilty of the more serious overarching racketeering charge in a landmark case in the US.

In a two-month trial, prosecutors accused the Chinese technology and telecommunications giant of lying, cheating and stealing to gain any competitive advantage.

Huawei's defence team argued that prosecutors had decided Huawei was a criminal organisation and built its case from there, but the firm's success was due to its own innovation.

Huawei said, external the racketeering acquittal "confirms that these allegations lack factual basis", adding that it would "take all necessary legal actions to defend our rights and interests".

Jurors in a Brooklyn court found Huawei guilty of 10 out of 12 counts, which included conspiracy to violate US sanctions.

However, the jury declined to convict Huawei of the racketeering conspiracy charge, which required members to find the firm acted like a criminal enterprise.

Federal prosecutors first announced the charges against Huawei in 2019, after the dramatic arrest in late 2018 of senior executive Meng Wanzhou in Canada.

Meng, also known as Cathy Meng, was held for three years in Canada before she was released to return to China through a deal with US prosecutors.

During closing arguments last week, assistant US attorney Meredith Arfa told jurors Huawei spent 20 years doing whatever it took to expand its business, including lying to banks about its business dealings in Iran and stealing technology secrets from rival firms to ensure it had a competitive edge.

"For decades, Huawei and three of its subsidiaries conducted their business in the United States on the basis of lies and theft," US Attorney General Todd Blanche said in statement, external.

"Thanks to the work of the FBI and our federal prosecutors, those who engaged in these criminal activities will be held accountable."

However, defence attorney Brian Heberlig said it was by dint of hard work that Huawei's business had expanded with operations in 170 countries and more than 200,000 staff.

Following the verdict, Heberlig told the BBC that Huawei was gratified that the jury had rejected the racketeering charge.

"The RICO [Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act] count that alleged that Huawei was a corrupt organisation, with a corporate culture and policy of committing crime. That was, in our view, a significant overreach, and the jury soundly rejected it," he said.

"Of course, we have mixed emotions about the rest of the verdict ... [we] thought that Huawei was deserving of acquittals across the board."

Sentencing is planned for September 2027. Heberlig said there will be "significant advocacy" to achieve the lowest sentence possible. Following that, he said he fully expects Huawei to appeal.

"If I know Huawei, that fight will continue, and the company's not going to rest until it's pursued every last argument it can through appeal, and hopefully, ultimately toward vindication," he said.
